# Preface

The rapid advancement of artificial intelligence (AI) and its integration with healthcare have resulted in significant improvements in medical diagnosis, patient care, and treatment regimens. As healthcare systems increasingly rely on data-driven insights, AI has emerged as a critical tool for improving the precision, efficiency, and reach of medical services. The book *Artificial Intelligence for Healthcare: Machine Learning and Diagnostic* aims to explain the technicalities of AI applications in numerous medical domains, providing a road map for healthcare professionals and researchers.

The chapters have been carefully chosen to cover a wide range of AI-driven advancements across disciplines, including cardiovascular disease diagnosis and orthopedic management, remote patient monitoring, emergency medicine, etc. Each chapter delves into the various applications of AI and machine learning algorithms in healthcare administration, enhancing patient outcomes, and enabling quicker, more precise diagnosis. The book also discusses the various applications of AI in pathology, surgery, dentistry, elder care, and even prenatal care, demonstrating the technology’s adaptability and capacity to customize treatment plans.

The need to bridge the knowledge gap between theoretical and practical AI applications in healthcare motivated the authors to write this book. As AI becomes increasingly prevalent in clinical settings, healthcare workers must not only accept the technologies in their utilization but also adopt the paradigm shift that AI represents in healthcare management. The book hopes to be a beneficial resource for professionals, educators, researchers, and students interested in using AI to improve healthcare outcomes.

The book *Artificial Intelligence for Healthcare: Machine Learning and Diagnostic* might inspire applications of AI technology, ultimately boosting the medical sector and improving patient outcomes globally.

# Foreword

The emergence of artificial intelligence (AI) has triggered a tremendous revolution in the healthcare industry. As we approach a new era in medicine, AI has the potential to transform the method to diagnose, treat, and manage diseases. Personalized treatment plans, more accurate diagnoses, and data-driven decision-making are all made possible by the advances in AI.

In this perspective, the book *Artificial Intelligence for Healthcare: Machine Learning and Diagnostic*, written by Dr. Rishabha Malviya and his team, explores the application of AI in various healthcare domains, including cardiovascular disease diagnosis, remote patient monitoring, emergency medicine, and surgical interventions. The book also explores the theoretical and practical applications of AI, highlighting its potential to enhance efficiency, accuracy, and patient outcomes. The book discusses the function of AI not just in traditional clinical settings, but also in developing fields such as pathology, dentistry, elder care, and maternity health, demonstrating AI’s immense potential across the medical spectrum.

As healthcare systems around the world face resource restrictions, aging populations, and expanding healthcare needs, AI provides a strong solution to optimize healthcare management. The approaches presented in this book are well-suited to assisting healthcare practitioners, researchers, and academicians in understanding these issues, ultimately leading to improved healthcare outcomes and lower costs.

I applaud Dr. Rishabha Malviya for compiling this knowledgeable list, which will be a helpful resource for anybody trying to grasp the present and potential applications of AI in healthcare. The book not only reflects on past advances but also looks ahead to future developments that will transform the medical environment. As AI evolves and expands its capabilities, the knowledge offered in this book will help prepare healthcare practitioners for the future of AI-driven medical practice.

Happy reading and best wishes.

![](graphic/converted/b_9783111549729_ingr_001.jpg)

**Dr. Dhruv Galgotia**

CEO, Galgotias University,

Greater Noida, India

# Chapter 1 Cardiovascular disease diagnosis using AI-based imaging

#### Abstract

Heart disease (cardiovascular disease (CVD)) is still the leading cause of illness and death across the world, even though there has been a lot of improvement in diagnosing and treating it. By improving and optimizing outcomes for CVD, artificial intelligence (AI) methods could make a big difference in the field of cardiology, especially in the area of imaging. These techniques give new ways to look at data and make clinical decisions, which has completely changed the field of cardiology. Machine learning, deep learning, and other forms of AI could help doctors learn more by processing big, complicated datasets more efficiently and finding useful clinical insights. Modern imaging tools and methods are now very important in the area of diagnostic digital radiology for figuring out what’s wrong with patients and how to treat them. The use of digital acquisition and analysis is a key part of improving diagnostic skills, especially in the field of cardiovascular imaging. Along with this, adding AI to digital cardiovascular imaging greatly increases the field’s growth potential and its ability to make complete and accurate diagnoses of CVDs. More and more, AI is being used to look at different kinds of cardiovascular imaging, such as nuclear cardiac imaging, cardiovascular computed tomography, cardiovascular magnetic resonance imaging, and more. Everyday life has been greatly affected by technological progress, and more and more people are choosing to use commercial smart personal gadgets to keep track of their health. This chapter talks about the possible benefits of using AI technologies to help control CVD. It focuses on how these technologies could lead to better patient care. This chapter delineates the fundamental engineering principles that underlie the widespread use of ubiquitous sensors, as well as their potential sources of error. It also talks about how these devices can be used to remotely check for and diagnose common heart problems like arrhythmias, as well as how they can be used to help people who already have heart problems like heart failure.

Keywords:  Artificial intelligence,  heart failure,  monitoring,  sensors,  imaging,  cardiology,  

## 1.1 Introduction

Recent progress in technology has led to the creation of many inventions that are meant to make medical evaluation and treatment better. The use of artificial intelligence (AI) improves many types of technologies. In medicine, AI improves treatment planning by making it more accurate. Physicians can quickly figure out the best ways to treat patients who are showing certain signs. AI’s enhanced precision and automaticity capabilities contribute to a decrease in human error. AI has the potential to benefit patients by providing guidance through phone applications. The severity of the patient’s illness will decide if the patient needs immediate medical assistance at an emergency department or approach their local doctor’s office. Patients can use these apps to better understand their symptoms and figure out the best way to treat them. Many types of heart imaging have been made better with the help of AI. These include echocardiography, cardiovascular computed tomography, nuclear cardiac imaging, and cardiovascular magnetic resonance imaging.

## 1.2 AI: general medical applications

AI enables technological evolution that enhances the existing capabilities. The objective of AI is to develop computer-based intelligence capable of problem-solving and task performance, thereby mimicking human cognitive abilities \[[1](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_001)\]. AI encompasses the creation of algorithms that emulate human calculating abilities to systematically solve problems and deduce information. AI has been used a lot in many areas and for many different purposes. To improve treatment and diagnosis methods as well as the efficiency of healthcare management \[[2](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_002)\], AI has been used in the field of medicine. A lot of the time, healthcare notes have a lot of messy, hard-to-find information. When doctors use AI, they can get full medical knowledge about their patients in real time, which helps them make better medical decisions. Using electronic medical records is made a lot easier by algorithms that let one look for people who have a strong family history of a problem or a tendency to get it over and over again \[[3](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_003)\]. Personalized medicine and early disease detection are made easier when medical data are well-organized.

A number of fields have made use of AI, such as algorithm development and the creation of seeming things like robots. Robots are often used to help surgeons do operations on patients \[[4](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_004)\]. Because it allows for less invasive cuts, robotic help in surgery greatly improves the quality of care. This helps people feel less pain after surgery and get better faster. The robotic surgery tool makes dissection, cutting, and suturing more accurate. AI allows surgery robots study and understand how a surgeon moves during an operation, which allows the robot copy and carry out these actions on its own \[[5](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_005)\]. Additionally, using robots in surgery greatly reduces the number of mistakes that people make, like those caused by shaky hands or cutting themselves accidentally \[[6](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_006)\].

## 1.3 Introduction to artificial intelligence

There are some computer systems that can do things that normally require the intelligence of a human being. This is called “artificial intelligence.”. Some of the methods used for such tasks are algorithms, pattern matching, heuristics, cognitive computing, rules, and deep learning (DL). They involve taking in information from outside sources and acting on it.

Clinical practitioners are interested in coming up with new, reliable, and effective ways to provide high-quality health care because AI technologies are improving so quickly, especially in the areas of machine learning (ML) and DL. In cardiovascular medicine, AI is mostly used for imaging study and development. There are many benefits to using AI models to help control cardiovascular disease (CVD). For example, they reduce variation between and within operators and give predictive information that individuals might not be able to see \[[7](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_007), [8](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_008)\].

Patients who might have coronary artery disease (CAD) can benefit from using AI in cardiac uses. In clinical practice, using cardiac computed tomography (CT) along with ML algorithms has shown potential for helping people who are having heart problems. It makes it possible to use noninvasive methods and find useful information in addition to describing atherosclerotic plaque \[[9](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_009), [10](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_010)\]. ML could be used in cardiology to automatically find problems in electrocardiograms (ECGs), in addition to diagnostic images.

### 1.3.1 Machine learning

A branch of AI called ML tries to make it possible for computers to analyze very large datasets by using complicated computer and statistical methods. These algorithms are capable of recognizing patterns in new data that are consistent with the patterns they have acquired from previous data. Because of this, they can use these trends to make predictions.

A functional relationship *y* = *f*(*x*) shows the connection between an input *x* and an output *y* in the field of ML. This relationship is also called the prediction model \[[11](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_011)\]. [Figure 1.1](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_fig_001) shows how ML is categorized.

![Figure 1.1: The classification of machine learning.](graphic/converted/b_9783111549729-001_fig_001.png)

Figure 1.1: The classification of machine learning.

According to the predictive model’s methodology for data acquisition and processing, ML can be divided into three categories \[[12](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_012), [13](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_013), [14](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_014), [15](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_015), [16](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_016)\]. The first category of methods in ML, namely supervised learning, utilizes datasets that have been labeled by humans. Support vector machines, neural networks, and logistic regression are some examples of these types of methods. These techniques are often used to create models that can predict or group future events and figure out which factors are most important for getting the desired result. The factors x and y are known, and training the predictive model with data makes it better and gives it more benefits. Unsupervised learning, which includes cluster analysis, is the second type of ML. In this approach, the software identifies patterns in datasets without relying on predefined categories in the training set. The only known information consists of the input variables (*x*). It possesses the capacity to reveal novel associations within the dataset. Reinforcement learning, a type of reward-based learning, is frequently utilized in video games and robotic applications. To improve the predictive model, the process involves interacting with a setting and using both positive and negative reinforcements. The machine requires various systems and tools, including cameras, sensors, and GPS, to improve its learning abilities and understand its surroundings.

### 1.3.2 Deep learning

Neural networks are used in DL, which is a type of guided ML. The system is unique because it has automated algorithms that can find trends in large amounts of data \[[17](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_017)\]. Like the human brain, the artificial neural network can use different levels of abstraction to build complex hierarchical representations from data \[[18](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_018)\]. When the coder gives the computer data, they do it in a way that allows algorithms respond correctly to the new information. The neural network acquires knowledge by processing data, constructing hierarchical structures, and generating sophisticated input-output relationships. It is able to recognize complex nonlinear correlations between the variables that serve as inputs and those that serve as outputs. To minimize the discrepancy between observed outcomes and their corresponding predictions, it is possible to estimate the relative importance of input and outcome data \[[19](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_019)\].

When making diagnoses, medical professionals rely on their knowledge, experience, and the cultural context of their patients. DL has the ability to dramatically improve medical knowledge, particularly for physicians who are not experts in their subject, which would lead to tremendous progress in this area.

## 1.4 Electronic health: mobile health and IoT

eHealth, often known as digital health, is the application of advancement of technology for communication and information, most notably the Internet, with the intention of improving the quality of health care \[[20](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_020)\].

### 1.4.1 Mobile health

mHealth is a type of eHealth that uses mobile and wireless tools to make access to medical care better. There is a lot of promise for mobile devices to help lower CVD. This is especially true for healthy self-monitoring apps, activity monitors, and blood pressure (BP) monitors. These applications have the potential to assist patients in achieving a healthy weight, enhancing movement or exercise, quitting smoking habits, regulating glucose levels of the blood, and managing blood pressure and lipids to reach desired levels \[[21](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_021), [22](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_022)\].

The growing importance of mHealth in the area of cardiovascular medicine can be attributed to the progress made in movable computer systems and small heart imaging devices. The combination of “big data” from mHealth as well as telemedicine with AI has the capability to enhance clinical decision-making for cardiologists \[[23](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_023)\].

### 1.4.2 Internet of Things

There is a network called the Internet of Things (IoT) that connects gadgets, cars, buildings, and other things that have software, sensors, electronics, and network connections. This network facilitates data collection and sharing \[[24](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_024)\]. IoT medical apps usually have three layers: the sense layer has sensors that patients can wear or attach to them; the transport layer has connectors; and the application layer has a remote server \[[25](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_025)\]. The collected data is either saved in a database on a remote server or provided in real time by healthcare providers. The data is delivered to a remote server.

Telemedicine is advantageous for older individuals and those with serious heart conditions, as it shifts the medical sector from a passive to a widespread approach. In the field of cardiac disease management, doctors can continuously have surveillance over patients by collecting data on blood pressure, ECG, and SpO[2]. This allows them to stay informed about the patient’s health status and make diagnoses or predictions regarding potential risks. This advancement will significantly improve patients’ quality of life by enabling them to lead more typical lives. They will receive medical care in their own homes, resulting in reduced hospital visits. Combining IoT techniques with real-time analytical algorithms enables the potential for early detection and warning of potential attacks.

## 1.5 AI-based general application in CVD

### 1.5.1 Precision medicine

For patients, AI can be used for follow-ups that happen over the internet, medicine reminders, real-time disease counseling, and finding symptoms early on. AI can help clinicians by collecting voice data, such as medical background, integrating electronic medical records systems, and making their work easier \[[26](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_026)\]. Cognitive computers, which are trained with ML or DL algorithms, might be able to help doctors make good choices and guess how patients will do in the future. AI can be used to make exact and customized plans for each patient’s medical care. There is a prevailing belief that AI will not supplant clinicians in their roles. It is recommended that doctors learn how to use AI technology well and gain experience in using AI in the real world to improve the detection and treatment of CVD by analyzing large datasets. This will expedite the emergence of precision medicine. Using AI is thought to be a key part of making healthcare more personalized for each patient, which is also known as precision medicine. There is an assumption that clinicians will not be replaced by AI.

### 1.5.2 Clinical predictions

ML and big data analytics can be used by AI to improve clinical results. The study by Dawes T.J.W. suggests that AI might be able to predict when people with heart disease will die \[[27](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_027)\]. Researchers involved the utilization of AI software to collect data from 256 heart disease patients, specifically recording the outcomes of cardiac MRI scans and blood tests. The software quantified the motion of 30,000 points located on the heart structures during each cardiac cycle. AI might be able to predict abnormal conditions that could lead to patient death by combining this data with the patients’ 8-year medical information. Also, their software was able to predict how many patients would still be alive after 5 years, with an impressive 80% accuracy in predicting life for the next year. The doctors were 60% accurate in their predictions. Researchers made a DL-based prediction model to look at the 5-year risk of death for 10,030 people who were thought to have coronary heart disease. The results show that AI-based risk assessment works better than both standard clinical judgment and coronary CT angiography \[[28](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_028)\].

### 1.5.3 Intelligent robots

Surgical robots have made it easier for doctors to do treatments like hysteromyoma resection and bladder replacement surgery \[[29](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_029), [30](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_030)\]. The Da Vinci Surgical Robot is an example of how AI can be used with minimally invasive surgical technology to make automated treatment more likely in the future. This advancement has the potential to minimize patient trauma, enhance surgical safety, and expedite hospital discharge. This combination also allows AI perform cardiac interventional treatments like percutaneous coronary intervention and catheter ablations for atrial fibrillation (AF), which means that doctors do not have to be exposed to as much radiation as they do during digital subtraction angiography. Reinforcement learning can enhance AI capabilities, surpassing those of humans, particularly in repetitive drill tasks. The AI will acquire the ability to perform operations more efficiently than human clinicians. Putting AI and surgical tools together will make it easier for traditional medicine to change.

## 1.6 Other medical applications of AI in CVD

### 1.6.1 Cardiovascular imaging

AI is valuable in the field of cardiology due to its ability to effectively integrate and correlate information from various sources, facilitating efficient interpretation by physicians \[[31](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_031)\]. CAD is a common cardiovascular disorder, accounting for 20% of all fatalities \[[32](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_032)\]. AI methods like ML, DL, and cognitive computing could totally change how cardiology and cardiovascular medicine are performed. ML can employ diverse methodologies to analyze large volumes of data. This is specifically evident in the field of cardiac imaging, where these techniques can significantly impact knowledge generation, data interpretation, and decision-making processes. [Figure 1.2](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_fig_002) depicts various AI-based cardiovascular imaging applications.

![Figure 1.2: Cardiac imaging applications.](graphic/converted/b_9783111549729-001_fig_002.png)

Figure 1.2: Cardiac imaging applications.

### 1.6.2 Echocardiography

Echocardiography is essential for the diagnosis and treatment of CVDs, as well as providing precise quantitative evaluations of cardiac structures and functions. Nevertheless, the outcome is contingent upon the variability and expertise of the interoperators. AI tools, specifically ML, offer opportunities to improve the precision of the analysis of images in clinical echocardiography, particularly among untrained clinicians. Models of ML trained to analyze images can effectively identify various disease patterns by considering the individual pixels and their interconnections. ML models possess the potential to automatically interpret the unutilized data produced by the emergence of multidimensional imaging techniques like 3D echocardiography. This leads to a reduction in the amount of time spent on analysis and an increase in accuracy \[[33](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_033)\].

Echocardiography is a diagnostic technique that does not include any kind of invasive procedure, and it is used to identify and track the growth of cardiovascular illnesses. The ability to visualize the anatomy, functioning, and hemodynamics of the heart, in addition to any abnormalities that are characteristic of the patient, offers a number of benefits. Echocardiography is a quick and accurate way to diagnose heart problems, and it has never been linked to any bad outcomes \[[34](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_034)\]. It’s also not expensive. Echocardiography does have a flaw, though: how accurate it is depends on how the doctor interprets the pictures. This is a very important limitation. Even though ultrasound can take pictures, there is still a chance of getting the wrong diagnosis. This problem can be solved by AI, which can analyze echocardiograms in a way that is exact, reliable, and organized. Because of this, mistakes made by individuals are less likely to happen, and it’s now easier for doctors to come up with effective treatment plans. The algorithms that were made by AI can correctly spot many different illnesses, including valvopathies and ischemia in coronary artery disease. The use of AI to improve the accuracy of echocardiograms for diagnosis is still in its early stages, and study is still being done to make it even more useful \[[35](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_035)\]. On echocardiography, it can be hard to tell the difference between a number of diseases. ML techniques help improve workflow and cut down on mistakes that doctors make when they are tired and interpreting images.

Echocardiography can make it hard to tell the difference between a number of illnesses. Researchers \[[7](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_007)\] are looking into how ML can be used in imaging to tell the difference between hypertrophic cardiomyopathy and an athlete’s heart. ML-based algorithms help improve workflow and lower the number of mistakes that happen when doctors are tired and analyzing images. A convolutional neural network (CNN) method was used by researchers to look at 267 echocardiogram images, each of which had 15 standard views. Labeled images were used to teach the algorithm. The study showed that the model was 97.8% accurate at identifying the echocardiogram view, while expert echocardiographers were only 70.2–84% accurate \[[36](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_036)\].

### 1.6.3 Magnetic resonance imaging

Heart problems can be checked out with a cardiac magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), which does not involve any invasive procedures. The most accurate way to find out the ejection percentage and the volume of the left ventricle is usually agreed to be MRI. ML models can be used in a lot of different ways with cardiac MRI in the area of ventricular segmentation. It makes it possible to quantify measures of volume and makes clinical evaluations more accurate and dependable \[[37](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_037)\]. The ML method is also used in cardiac MRI to find mild or chronic myocardial scar \[[38](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_038)\].

The researchers found a good link between the results of the DL system and the results of the human analysis when they looked at the left and right ventricular volumes, filling, and ejection rates \[[39](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_039)\]. The results of automatic tests made with DL were very similar to the results of tests that were interpreted by a person. The use of DL can improve the reconstruction of cardiac pictures, which can lead to better 3D visualization for finding disease patterns in the right ventricle. This is due to the limited visibility of the right ventricle in two-dimensional echocardiography. Using DL, different features can be easily extracted, like the right ventricle and pulmonary artery hypertension \[[40](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_040), [41](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_041)\].

In the past few years, cardiac MRI has become more common as a noninvasive imaging method. The results of this technique are photographs of the heart’s cross section that are symmetrical with respect to its axis. The need for extensive knowledge of cardiac anatomy poses an additional challenge for medical imaging experts when interpreting MRI results. In the past, it was thought that the heart was in the middle of the picture, but now it is known that different patients’ hearts are shaped differently. Researchers proposed the use of CNN, a deep neural network approach, to accurately identify and locate regions of interest in MRI scans. The CNN was taught to find the object in the picture and tell the difference between pixels that are in the background and pixels that are in the enclosing box. The neural network in question solved the problem by treating it like a classification problem: it had to tell the difference between pixels that were part of the background and pixels that were part of a box \[[42](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_042)\].

### 1.6.4 Cardiac computed tomography

CT and other imaging methods are very useful for finding and treating heart problems. With a CT scan, pictures of the heart can be made in more than one plane, and they can also be made in three dimensions.

ML image processing methods are often used in cardiac CT to diagnose CAD and figure out how likely it is that someone will develop atherosclerosis. Some of these tests are the fractional flow estimate and the coronary artery calcium scoring. Coronary computed tomographic angiography, or CCTA, is a way to find out if someone has coronary artery disease that does not involve any kind of surgery. It tends to overstate how bad the stenosis is compared to passive angiography. Using fractional flow reserve (FFR) as a guide, the presence of angiographic stenosis does not always mean that it is important for hemodynamics \[[43](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_043)\]. Several ML models have been made \[[44](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_044)\] to figure out how to correctly classify noninvasive FFR and make CCTA more accurate by finding stenosis that do not have a big effect on the patient’s blood flow.

When doctors are checking out people who might have CAD, CT imaging is useful because it allows them see how much calcium and plaque are in the coronary arteries without having to do any invasive treatments. Because of this, CT scans are a useful way to check out people who might have CAD. The fact that this happened indicates the buildup of plaque has made the arteries narrow or block \[[45](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_045)\]. The calcium score, which counts how much calcium is in the blood vessels, can help show how bad CAD is and what the future holds for it. ML has been used to correctly find a number of diseases on CT scan \[[46](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_046)\].

ML is also used to help make low-dose CT scans safer. People who use low-dose CT imaging are worried that patients will be exposed to more radiation. This problem cannot be fixed by just dropping the radiation levels, as that would lower the quality of the images \[[47](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_047)\]. These concerns cannot be addressed by simply reducing radiation levels because doing so would undermine the quality of the photographs. To solve this problem, a ML architecture was made that reconstructs picture parameters and boosts image quality in low-radiation situations. This improved the quality of the pictures to the same level as regular-dose CT images, so patients could get a diagnosis with less radiation exposure \[[48](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_048)\].

### 1.6.5 Electrocardiography

It would be helpful to use ML techniques in the area of electrocardiography as well as diagnostic imaging. An ECG is often used to find problems with the heart’s electrical activity. ECGs can now automatically find problems because of ML models, especially DL models. This has resulted in decreased interpretation time and reduced reliance on individual variability \[[49](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_049), [50](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_050)\].

### 1.6.6 Nuclear cardiology

Nuclear cardiology makes use of technologies that are not invasive in order to evaluate cardiac blood flow. This test is very useful for finding coronary artery disease and likely ischemia. Ischemia happens when the heart does not get enough oxygen because blood flow is low. Cardiovascular SPECT and PET-CT are the two types of tests that can be done in nuclear cardiology. Both of these tests are done with an intravenous shot, and then a PET scan is made \[[51](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_051)\]. Radioactive materials are put into the bloodstream during both of these treatments. For example, AI could be useful in nuclear cardiology to fix the problems with SPECT images that make it hard to spot problems \[[52](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_052)\]. DL enhances image analysis by effectively detecting complex patterns in high-dimensional data.

### 1.6.7 Angiography imaging

Invasive angiography-based imaging is another commonly employed imaging procedure in the area of cardiology. Many people think that coronary angiography is the best way to find out if someone has obstructive coronary artery disease because it shows the full shape and function of the heart’s blood vessels. Even though it is useful, invasive angiography comes with hazards such as the possibility of major consequences, high expenses, possible radiation exposure, and discomfort \[[53](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_053)\]. DL  possesses the capability to improve diagnostic imaging tools, ensuring patient safety and improving the accuracy of results \[[54](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_054)\].

In addition, CCTA is a diagnostic technique for coronary artery disease. Despite its potential for ruling out CAD, the CCTA method has several limitations. These include an inclination to overestimate vessel stenosis and a lengthy turnaround time for obtaining results. Introducing AI to CCTA could make it better by making it easier to check for coronary stenosis, plaque characteristics, and the level of cardiac ischemia \[[55](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_055)\].

More and more people are using the ML-based FFR-CTTA to find out if someone has CAD. FFR-CTTA is a safe way to make a three-dimensional picture of the coronary arteries of a patient \[[56](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_056)\]. The FFR is a way to compare the highest blood flow that can happen in healthy coronary arteries to the highest blood flow that can happen in sick coronary arteries. An FFR number of 1.0 is normal, but a value below 0.75–0.80 is linked to myocardial ischemia \[[57](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_057)\].

### 1.6.8 Intravascular imaging

Intravascular imaging uses catheter-based techniques like intravascular ultrasound (IVUS) or optical coherence tomography (OCT) to get a real-time picture of the inside of a coronary artery. MRI and nuclear MRI are two other methods that use catheters. Additionally, it gives a visual representation of the artery’s inner opening and details about how severely an artery is narrowed or stopped. IVUS is often used to get a better idea of the make-up of artery plaque and where to put stents \[[58](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_058)\]. For this imaging method to work, catheters must be put into the coronary arteries. This is a difficult, risky process that requires a high level of technical skill. AI has been used by researchers to speed up the diagnosis and interpretation of blood images in real time. The AI algorithm is instructed by a series of photographs, and it is provided with information regarding each image. This information shows the shape of the tube and where different kinds of tissue are located. As it is given more chances to do so and sees more images, the AI system gets better at reading intravascular imaging and making accurate judgments.

IVUS imaging goes deeper into the tissue than OCT imaging, which has a better resolution but less tissue penetration. Even though they are different, AI can combine the data of different intravascular imaging methods. AI combines data from imaging methods like IVUS and OCT into a single imaging method. This makes it easy for doctors to look at all of the data at the same time \[[59](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_059)\]. This plan has many advantages, but the main one is that it allows for a faster and more thorough examination of any damaged vessels.

## 1.7 AI-based wearables in cardiovascular care

### 1.7.1 Heart rate and rhythm sensors

The heart rate (HR) measurements taken while the person is at rest and while they are active can both help predict their chance of getting CVDs. In groups of people who are healthy, having a high resting HR has been linked to a higher chance of coronary artery disease and death in general \[[60](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_060)\]. Also, it is often seen as a sign that a person with heart failure (HF) will not have a good result \[[61](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_061)\]. Bad cardiovascular events are more likely to happen if the HR does not recover quickly enough during activity \[[62](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_062)\]. Researchers have found a strong link between the heart rate’s viability and the chance of bad cardiovascular events in both healthy people and people with HF who have a lower ejection fraction \[[63](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_063)\].

ECG or photoplethysmography (PPG) is used in commercial gadgets to measure heart rhythm and HR. By figuring out the time between beats, these gadgets use algorithms to classify heart rhythm. Many people agree that using ECG monitors, which come in different shapes and sizes, are the standard way to check HR and rhythm. Chest-strap monitors and ECG patches can continuously record the heart rate, but customers do not like them as much as smartwatches because they are bulky, do not have as many features, and are inconvenient to use for a long time. A single-lead ECG can be recorded on some smartwatches by just touching the top of the watch. The negative electrode is on the outer edge of the watch, and the positive electrode is on the back \[[64](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_064)\]. Single-lead ECGs are useful for diagnosing common arrhythmias, including AF. Single-lead ECGs might not be enough to accurately diagnose complex arrhythmias, myocardial infarction (MI), or interval changes if certain procedures are not used \[[65](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_065)\].

PPG measures changes in the amount of blood in small blood vessels. These changes are then turned into pulse waves and recorded as a tachogram. The emitter sends out a steady stream of photons, and the photodetector counts the changing strength of photons that are reflected from the tissue \[[66](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_066)\]. During activity, wearables normally always maintain the PPG sensor active. However, to preserve battery power, PPG measurements are only recorded sporadically when the wearable is being used for rest or sleep. PPG tachograms could detect arrhythmias \[[67](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_067)\], particularly when utilized in conjunction with a single-lead ECG.

[Figure 1.3](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_fig_003) shows a list of common smart wearable tech items, the sensors they have built-in, and how they can be used in cardiovascular healthcare. [Table 1.1](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_tab_001) presents a comprehensive compilation of prevalent wearable products available in the market, along with the corresponding published studies and regulatory status associated with each product.

8.  Heart failure management
    
9.  Medication titration such as β-blockers
    

 |
| ECG || Single-lead and multi-lead ECG, continuous or as-needed ECG monitoring, interval measurements such as QTc, arrhythmia detection and electrolyte abnormality changes |
| Oscillometer || Wrist-cuff BP |
| **Others** |
| Biochemical sensors || Invasive for continuous blood glucose and electrolyte monitoring  
Noninvasive for sweat and saliva electrolyte and hydration status || 

1.  Identifying electrolyte abnormalities
    
2.  Continuous blood glucose monitoring
    
3.  Heart failure management
    

 |
| Biochemical sensors such as ballistocardiograms, seismocardiograms and dielectric sensors || Cardiac output, stroke volume, lung-fluid volume, body vibrations, weight |

This image gives an outline of smart wearables that are currently on the market. It describes where they are worn on the body, the sensors that are built in, the kinds of data that each sensor takes, and how they are used in cardiovascular clinical settings. A heart disease is called CVD, an electrocardiogram is called an ECG, and GPS stands for the Global Positioning System. Heart rate (HR), heart rate recovery (HRR), heart rate variability (HRV), photoplethysmography (PPG), and oxygen saturation (SaO[2]) are all terms used in cardiovascular science.

### 1.7.2 Monitoring for hypertension

Early screening for hypertension is widely recommended in young adulthood as a preventive measure against heart-related disease \[[68](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_068)\]. Oscillometric or cuff-less wearables that properly measure BP and are constantly worn on the wrist may offer more convenience in the ambulatory situation compared to standard upper arm BP devices. This is because oscillometric or cuff-less wearables do not need the user to remove their arm from the device. These wearables can be used for screening hypertension, self-monitoring BP, and adjusting antihypertensive medications \[[69](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_069)\].

Continuous BP monitoring with new sensors has the potential to provide BP measurement during activities such as exercise, when oscillometric measures may not be viable \[[70](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_070)\]. This has the potential to enable BP monitoring during sleep, which is currently not possible.

### 1.7.3 Monitoring of atrial fibrillation and other arrhythmias

The worldwide prevalence of AF, as well as its link with stroke, HF, and death, have both been well reported \[[71](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_071)\]. Wearables act as a convenient diagnostic instrument that can detect asymptomatic or symptomatic AF \[[72](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_072)\].

Over the course of 8 months, the Apple Heart study enlisted the participation of 419,297 people from the United States. This makes it the largest study to date that was carried out remotely. Its objective was to determine whether a PPG-enabled device could accurately detect AF in individuals who did not have a previous diagnosis of the disease. After identifying irregularities in the initial tachogram, the algorithm proceeded to detect irregularities in the PPG signal during less arm movement. A notification was sent to the participant’s Apple Watch as well as the study app if the study app and Apple Watch both detected four consecutive irregular tachograms. This informed the participant to the possibility of an irregular pulse. An algorithm that identifies irregular heartbeats had an 84% positive predictive value for AF diagnosis, compared to 71% of individual tachograms \[[73](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_073)\].

### 1.7.4 Monitoring of heart failure

Risk stratification in HF patients can be achieved by the use of wearable data, which includes levels of physical activity, HR, heart rate variability (HRV), and heart rate recovery. Dielectric sensors, among other emerging biomechanical sensors, show promise for managing heart failure. However, their clinical adoption necessitates thorough evaluation. The efficacy of distant invasive and noninvasive telemonitoring therapy for HF patients has been investigated in multiple randomized clinical studies, with conflicting results \[[74](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_074)\].

## 1.8 AI and data management

It is common practice for patient records to be kept in separate databases across a variety of platforms and healthcare organizations. This makes it challenging to gain access to these records, which in turn may put patient safety at risk. To fully utilize wearables in patient care and research, it is crucial to establish semantic interoperability with platforms such as electronic health records and patient portals ([Figure 1.4](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_fig_004)).

![Figure 1.4: The integration of smart wearable data into clinical practice. This diagram illustrates the optimal integration of wearables in patient care. Wearable data, both raw and processed, can offer valuable clinical insights to healthcare professionals for assessing, diagnosing, and managing cardiovascular disease. In addition, by analyzing data from wearable devices, personalized, real time, and flexible health coaching solutions can be developed and sent to the individual. Transparent data user agreements allow for the safe storage of wearable data in personal health clouds or electronic health records (EHR), which allows for improved data processing, visualization, and sharing with third parties and research projects. (Created with BioRender.com)](graphic/converted/b_9783111549729-001_fig_004.png)

Figure 1.4: The integration of smart wearable data into clinical practice. This diagram illustrates the optimal integration of wearables in patient care. Wearable data, both raw and processed, can offer valuable clinical insights to healthcare professionals for assessing, diagnosing, and managing cardiovascular disease. In addition, by analyzing data from wearable devices, personalized, real time, and flexible health coaching solutions can be developed and sent to the individual. Transparent data user agreements allow for the safe storage of wearable data in personal health clouds or electronic health records (EHR), which allows for improved data processing, visualization, and sharing with third parties and research projects. (Created with BioRender.com)

The current guidelines must be updated to encourage hospitals, businesses, and other stakeholders to reach full interoperability while protecting the privacy and accessibility of patients’ data. Rather than being impeded by skepticism, it is essential to create these policies. It is essential to observe the preservation and provenance of large amounts of data, particularly in the generation of terabytes of medical data during the course of an individual’s lifetime. It is important to take into consideration the introduction of cutting-edge technology such as blockchain \[[75](#9783111547589_001324.xhtml#b_9783111549729-001_ref_075)\].

## 1.9 Conclusion

The utilization of AI has the capacity to improve and advance medical technologies, hence facilitating improved patient care. The approach has the capacity to enhance diagnostic accuracy, hence facilitating clinicians in the early detection of diseases and enabling them to strategize appropriate therapeutic interventions. Cardiology is one area where ML, a prominent branch of AI, has made great strides. The utilization of several imaging technologies is attributed to the comprehensive nature of patient workup. The field of AI is likely to make a lot of progress and grow over the next few years. These steps forward will be marked by AI systems getting better at making accurate diagnoses, which will improve the way decisions are made. This improvement might be because technology is always getting better and datasets used to make algorithms and find trends are getting bigger. When mHealth and telemedicine are combined, they open up new ways for patients and doctors to talk to each other. This changes healthcare from an inactive activity to an all-encompassing one. It is essential for physicians to adopt a receptive approach to the integration of AI in the field of cardiology in the future, rather than harboring dread. This is due to the fact that their specialized knowledge will remain indispensable, irrespective of the current situation. There are currently a lot of things that are stopping wearable devices from being widely used in clinical practice. As sensor and computer technology improve and make wearables more useful for various purposes, they will play a bigger role in cardiovascular practice. It is very important to set up thorough evaluation systems and proper regulatory tracking rules for these devices in order to make sure they are safe and work properly.
