# Chapter 2 Integration of AI in the management of bone health

#### Abstract

Artificial intelligence (AI) is a comprehensive concept that involves the use of computational systems to replicate intelligent behavior, thus reducing human intervention. AI in particular, has achieved substantial progress in perception tasks, enabling robots to more effectively represent and analyze intricate data. In the last few years, there has been a notable increase in the advancement and adoption of AI. Within the domain of orthopedics and traumatology, several investigations have been conducted employing AI techniques for detection of fracture. The research on AI pertaining to the detection and classification of fractures is now somewhat constrained. This chapter explores a concise explanation of the utilization of AI technology in context of fracture diagnosis, elucidating the many approaches and methodologies employed in this domain. AI has the capacity to improve healthcare, namely in the field of orthopedic surgery. The recent surge in research surrounding AI has generated optimism over the advancement of more effective risk stratification tools for personalizing orthopedics throughout all stages of care, ranging from diagnosis to therapy. In the foreseeable future, it is quite probable that AI-based technologies will provide assistance to orthopedic surgeons instead of displacing them. If computers take over everyday duties, physicians will have more time to practice medicine. Distinguishing between malignant and benign bone tumors presents complications in medical imaging. The expeditious advancement of AI methodologies has resulted in notable advancements in tasks related to picture identification, namely in the categorization and characterization of diverse cancers. This chapter will comprehensively examine the prospective clinical uses of AI systems in the diagnosis and treatment of bone disorders.

Keywords:  Osteoporosis,  bone fracture,  fracture detection,  artificial intelligence,  bone cancer,  

## 2.1 Introduction

Artificial intelligence (AI) is characterized as the utilization of computer systems to emulate intelligent behaviors, necessitating minimum human involvement \[[1](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_001)\]. AI, especially deep learning, has shown significant progress in detecting and interpreting complicated visual information \[[2](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_002)\].

Computer science has many areas of study, and one of them is AI \[[3](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_003), [4](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_004)\]. According to AI, the goal is to give machines, especially computers, the ability to do jobs as well as do them better than humans. More powerful computers, more space for data storage, and easier access to high-quality data have all sped up the growth of AI in healthcare. Methods based on AI are now used to solve many problems in orthopedics. These problems include identifying images, figuring out the risks of surgery before it happens, making professional decisions, and analyzing large datasets. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), computed tomography (CT), and other diagnostic services have become more popular in the past few years \[[5](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_005)\].

In recent years, there has been a big rise in the use of AI in imaging. In scientific studies about finding cancer, MRI and CT scans are the main methods used \[[6](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_006)\]. Pictures are taken as the first step in the medical imaging process. The second step is to recognize and tell the difference between different parts of the picture. This is similar to telling the difference between a plant’s stem and leaf by looking at their unique qualities. It is called “segmentation” in the field of imaging. People often call this process “ROI identification.” The next step is to pull out the important features. This is usually called “feature extraction and selection.” In the next step, different parts of the picture are put into groups \[[7](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_007)\]. Ensuring that the effects of AI on radiologists and patients are advantageous for all stakeholders is crucial ([Figure 2.1](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_fig_001)) \[[8](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_008)\].

![Figure 2.1: The different stages involved in the identification of cancer by image processing.](graphic/converted/b_9783111549729-002_fig_001.png)

Figure 2.1: The different stages involved in the identification of cancer by image processing.

Medical imaging and AI are very important for finding tumors early because they can spot the difference between cases that are benign and cases that are malignant. People who get care early have the best chances of survival \[[9](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_009)\].

In medicine, AI is useful for activities like figuring out what the symptoms mean and analyzing x-rays. Machine learning is used by AI to improve its ability over time through training. This process includes improving outcomes to make it easier to make decisions and make predictions. The process of learning includes several types \[[10](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_010)\]. Supervised learning is the process of making predictions about new data by working on data that already exists. After that, a classification method is used to categorize newly added data into different groups based on a learning model that already exists. Decisions are made using the process \[[11](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_011)\].

Deep learning (DL) is a field that shows how AI, especially machine intelligence, is becoming more prevalent. DL is a way of computing that uses a structure with many levels of networks that are all linked to each other. These networks are made to get the right features from the data that is fed into them. This makes awareness and precision more accurate and useful. Computer programs that use AI depend on DL techniques to do their processing and computing. Input layers and hidden layers make up neural networks, which are used for data processing and mathematical calculations \[[12](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_012)\]. DL is a way of computing that includes neural networks. Deep neural networks have become a powerful way to improve the accuracy and usefulness of diagnosing medical images.

## 2.2 Applications of AI in medical imaging

Cancer must be found and staged by doctors using MRI. Utilizing deep neural networks, which involve conducting a thorough investigation by adding an extra layer of max pooling and convolution, the study helped doctors find cancer. Photos showing cancer getting worse slowly were used in the study to find the exact stage at which a deep neural network can effectively spot the presence of cancer. By introducing early detection strategies, the study made a valuable contribution to the field of cancer by making it easier to find and treat the disease quickly. Identifying and addressing health problems early on were the main goals of the study \[[13](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_013)\]. In order to get the most out of radiomic data for practical purposes, the researchers recommended using AI and computer software. This is another reason why AI is being used in medicine: to classify diseases and make it easier to measure the effects of illnesses both locally and across large areas \[[14](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_014)\]. AI has the ability to analyze enormous volumes of medical images in an efficient manner, which allows it to identify disease attributes that may not be detected by human observers \[[15](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_015)\].

## 2.3 Application of AI in orthopedics

### 2.3.1 Natural language processing (NLP)

The field of DL has a specialized area called natural language processing (NLP) that studies how computers can understand and analyze human language. Neural networks are used in DL, which is a branch of ML. DL algorithms could directly recognize trends in data and figure out which features are important for categorizing input data on their own. The objective of NLP is to convert unstructured patient medical records, such as surgery reports, into structured data formats. This functionality allows for the retrieval of information regarding the existence or nonexistence of observations or results \[[16](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_016)\]. NLP has been employed in orthopedics to detect and recognize occurrences of surgery site infections within unstructured medical records. Predictive abilities of the current application have been demonstrated that surpass models that only rely on administrative data and are comparable to the human extraction procedure \[[17](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_017)\]. NLP has been employed in the field of hip arthroplasty to recognize and categorize periprosthetic femur fractures by identifying common data elements \[[18](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_018)\].

### 2.3.2 Clinical prediction rule

The area of orthopedics incorporates a range of predictive methods that are used for both diagnostic and prognostic outcome purposes. ML-based algorithms possess the capability to enhance efficiency of workflow in the emergency department (ED) specifically for orthopedic trauma cases by employing clinical prediction rules \[[19](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_019)\]. In one study, doctors sent patients who had symptoms consistent with a scaphoid fracture to the X-ray department for confirmation. Approximately 20% of patients with a negative radiograph exhibit a discernible scaphoid fracture \[[20](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_020)\]. The development of the Clinical Prediction Rule aims to aid doctors in the identification of patients who require advanced imaging techniques, including MRI and CT scans. Patients may see a 31% reduction in the frequency of requested advanced imaging and unnecessary casting procedures as a result of this regulation \[[21](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_021)\]. The use of the Ottawa Ankle Rules by physicians can aid in gathering of a patient’s medical background and carrying out a physical assessment, through the application of predictive clinical indicators that increase the probability of detecting a fracture. These rules also have the advantage of self-learning and correction. Hence, the application of ML algorithms has the potential to enhance classification of risks in advanced imaging for patients, leading to higher accuracy through the incorporation of feedback from the obtained results during algorithm updates.

### 2.3.3 Outcome calculator

Risk stratification in the field of orthopedics holds a vital significance in minimizing the impact of preoccupied surgeons and addressing inconsistencies in treatment approaches. The method shows potential for enhancing patients’ functionality and decreasing healthcare costs ([Figure 2.2](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_fig_002)). Slight adjustments in the everyday decision-making processes within the context of providing care to a large number of patients have the potential to provide notable progress in the field of public health as a whole \[[22](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_022), [23](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_023)\]. ML-based decision tools have been created and implemented in several domains within the field of orthopedics, encompassing arthroplasty, trauma, cancer, and spinal illnesses \[[24](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_024), [25](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_025)\]. Decision algorithms used in the field of orthopedic oncology have demonstrated an elevated degree of accuracy in estimating the preoperative survival rates of individuals afflicted with metastatic disease in the spinal or extremities regions. The tools that have been developed can improve personalized survival prediction within a range of 30 days to five years. Additionally, they can assist in making treatment decisions, including both surgical and nonsurgical options. The estimation of potential benefits associated with elective surgery in arthroplasty is of paramount significance to optimize treatment methods and minimize the occurrence of elective procedures that carry a significant individual risk of adverse outcomes \[[26](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_026)\]. A risk assessment method was developed for orthopedic trauma to forecast the probability of postoperative delirium following hip fracture surgery. The anticipated incorporation of the ML algorithm \[[27](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_027)\] is predicted to enhance the effectiveness of a screening strategy designed to identify persons at risk for suffering delirium. Further research is necessary to ascertain the clinical effectiveness of the latter tool, which will be investigated through clinical testing and implementation studies.

![Figure 2.2: The systematic clinical approach for individuals who are clinically suspected of sustaining a distal radius fracture. (Created with BioRender.com)](graphic/converted/b_9783111549729-002_fig_002.png)

Figure 2.2: The systematic clinical approach for individuals who are clinically suspected of sustaining a distal radius fracture. (Created with BioRender.com)

## 2.4 Workflow of ML in orthopedics

The technique utilized for the workflow of ML in orthopedics is contingent upon the predictive modeling framework proposed by Professor Steyerberg and his associates. This domain involves the advancement of applications across multiple areas, such as NLP, computer vision, and clinical decision support ([Figure 2.3](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_fig_003)).

![Figure 2.3: Diagram illustrating progression from a clinical problem to the subsequent implementation.](graphic/converted/b_9783111549729-002_fig_003.png)

Figure 2.3: Diagram illustrating progression from a clinical problem to the subsequent implementation.

### 2.4.1 Predictive modeling: development of an ML algorithm

Predictive modeling using ML may utilize data collected from various study designs. Retrospective, registry data, prospective, and nested case-control studies are considered appropriate methodologies for the purpose of prognostic modeling. However, it should be noted that cross-sectional and case-control methods have been found to be more suitable for diagnostic modeling, as indicated by previous research \[[28](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_028)\]. ML demonstrates optimal efficacy when employed on extensive datasets that undergo regular updates. Existing literature suggests that to attain optimal performance, a dataset should encompass at least 200 events and 200 nonevents \[[29](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_029)\]. The advancement of ML algorithms necessitated the use of a significant clinical database obtained from the American College of Surgeons National Surgical Quality Improvement Program. The algorithms used by the Surgical Outcomes and Risk Assessment Group (SORG) served a range of functions, including the prediction of delirium after hip fracture surgery of elderly person \[[30](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_030)\].

A function has been defined wherein an outcome variable, also known as the dependent variable, is determined by considering a distinct collection of attributes, also referred to as independent variables. The preliminary phase in the formulation of a clinical prediction rule or outcome calculator necessitates an evaluation of the relevance of various variables. This involves identifying and selecting the features that have the greatest contribution to the outcome variable, while considering their clinical significance. The variables examined in the study comprise a range of clinical and radiological results. The elements encompass patient demographics, mechanisms of trauma, classifications of fractures, intraoperative observations, and surgical interventions, including screw and/or plate fixation or arthroplasty. In the fields of NLP and computer vision, algorithms have the ability to autonomously detect patterns from data and make independent decisions regarding the selection of features for the purpose of classifying input data. These features can include edges, curves, and color, among others. The supervised ML method conducts a process of training and internal validation repeatedly until it achieves an ideal level of model performance. The dependent variable for the delirium hip fracture prediction tool was postoperative delirium. The program used independent variables that were easily accessible, obtained through an analysis of variable significance. These included age, preoperative delirium, functional ability, BMI, preoperative dementia, ASA class, the necessity for mobility assistance prior to surgery, and preoperative creatinine levels.

Researchers structured stepwise ABCD technique that was employed to evaluate the prediction efficacy of ML systems. The approach encompasses four distinct components: calibration-in-the-large, a method that assesses the model intercept (A); calibration slope (B), which quantifies the calibration of the model; differentiation, which is assessed through the concordance statistic (C); and clinical utility, which is evaluated using decision curve analysis (D) \[[31](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_031)\]. Moreover, the assessment of the comprehensive performance of the model, which includes differentiation, is conducted through a comparison of the Brier score of the model against that of the null model. Several commonly used classification techniques in ML include decision trees, logistic regression, naïve Bayes classification, support vector machines, and neural networks (refer to [Figure 2.4](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_fig_004)). Linear classifiers possess the desirable qualities of interpretability and efficiency with regards to their training speed. Nonlinear classifiers exhibit greater flexibility and are capable of capturing intricate patterns. However, in cases involving limited sample sizes, they are susceptible to overfitting. Logistic regression is a statistical method that utilizes a sigmoidal probability curve to model numerical data, enabling predictions to be made regarding binary outcomes by transforming the log odds. Classification trees, such as gradient boosted machines and random forests, employ flowchart-like structures for decision-making, allowing for easy comprehension and visualization. Artificial neural networks (ANNs) are created based on biological neural networks, especially those characterized by a feed-forward architecture that includes hidden layers and neurons. These networks typically require large amounts of data. Support vector machines (SVMs) utilize the concept of identifying a hyperplane within a three-dimensional (kernel) scatterplot to effectively separate a dataset into two distinct classes. SVMs exhibit strong performance, particularly on limited datasets. The naïve Bayes machine is a probabilistic model that can be represented as a Venn diagram to illustrate logical relationships. It is particularly effective when applied to smaller datasets and when categorical features are used, assuming a normal distribution.

![Figure 2.4: Classification algorithms.](graphic/converted/b_9783111549729-002_fig_004.png)

Figure 2.4: Classification algorithms.

There is no single solution for selecting the appropriate machine learning algorithm. The decision is made following the completion of a research inquiry, data preparation, and the construction of multiple models. The assessment of model performance is conducted by taking into account all metrics following the ABCD technique \[[32](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_032)\], while also incorporating the variable importance that holds the highest clinical significance. The successful creation of an ML-based algorithm designed to forecast the occurrence of delirium after hip fracture surgery has yielded a remarkably precise model that holds substantial therapeutic significance. This algorithm outperformed the conventional approach of screening all patients and utilized easily accessible variables.

### 2.4.2 External validation

The inclusion of external validation is crucial in assessing the algorithm’s performance and its suitability for implementation in clinical practice. External validation can be achieved by employing validation approaches that involve temporal, geographical, or full independence \[[33](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_033)\]. The significance of external validation is undeniable, as a model’s performance may vary among different populations, resulting in diminished reliability of an unvalidated algorithm \[[34](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_034)\]. The validation group for the present ML algorithms that have been externally validated was acquired through a retrospective analysis conducted at a prominent tertiary care hospital \[[35](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_035), [36](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_036)\]. The ML algorithm, which has been developed and internally validated, is used to assess model performance on a distinct validation dataset using the same metrics as mentioned earlier. Institutions may exhibit variations in their data format, field semantics, and level of data cleansing, despite using the same electronic health record (EHR) provider \[[37](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_037)\]. In subsequent investigations, the utilization of standardized data elements for prevalent data models might enhance the incorporation of data from many institutions and facilitate the worldwide validation of prognostic models. This approach promotes independent validation and enhances the overall support for research endeavors \[[38](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_038)\].

A discharge prediction tool for older individuals with hip fractures was implemented in Boston, Massachusetts, employing data obtained from the United States. The presence of divergent healthcare systems, disparities in care standards, and variations in treatment methodologies can pose challenges to the generalizability of research findings across different countries. Recalibration strategies may be employed in cases where external validation indicates limited generalizability \[[39](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_039)\]. During the process of recalibration, several elements of the model are altered and customized to reflect the characteristics of each research population. This involves modifying the model’s intercept as well as the effects of the variables \[[40](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_040)\].

### 2.4.3 Evaluation and implementation

If the external validity of the algorithm is confirmed, clinicians may utilize a web-based application to effectively integrate the algorithm into their practice. This application can assist in decision-making and prioritize actions accordingly. The ideal approach for formulating and validating a real-time clinical prediction rule, computer vision model, or outcome calculator entails utilizing the developed ML algorithm in conjunction with the clinical data consistently captured within electronic health record (EHR) systems. The integrated and automated calculation of derived predictions is provided to the clinician \[[41](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_041)\]. ML is an innovative methodology that improves the capabilities of data analysis. However, it is imperative that the use of ML aligns with the established principles governing the development of diagnostic tests as outlined in earlier studies \[[42](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_042)\].

The use of a computer and integration with EHR is necessary for ML applications. However, this requirement raises concerns regarding patient privacy and presents challenges in the implementation process. Physicians must access the application, input data, and subsequently resume using the EHR. The primary obstacle lies in integrating an ML-based decision support tool into the workflow of an EHR system. Furthermore, the unique attributes of ML-based software necessitate a regulatory framework that facilitates necessary advancements in treatment while prioritizing the safety of the algorithm \[[43](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_043)\].

### 2.4.4 Improvement of the algorithm: continuous self-learning

The increased magnitude of the dataset greatly improves the efficacy of ML models, in considering modifications in either methodology or the demographic of patients. The ongoing acquisition of data has a role in improving ML models, although its impact diminishes as time progresses \[[44](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_044)\]. ML algorithms have a significant advantage over decision rules in terms of their capacity to enhance model accuracy over time. The benefit is evident in multiple aspects, including but not limited to the early detection of diseases, enhanced accuracy in diagnosis, identification of novel discoveries or patterns, and the advancement of specific approaches to diagnostics and therapy.

## 2.5 Role of AI in bone fracture detection

The utilization of AI in the medical domain, particularly in the examination of X-ray pictures, commenced in 1960 \[[45](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_045)\]. The data was initially transformed into numerical representation for computational and analytical objectives. The development of AI was subsequently facilitated by the emergence of computer-aided detection. Over the course of time, significant advancements have been observed in computer processing capabilities, mostly attributed to the development and improvement of machine learning systems. These systems possess the remarkable capacity to acquire knowledge and enhance performance through experiential learning. ML is crucial to the progression of AI systems across multiple domains. AI models enhance the precision and effectiveness of results on datasets by means of training and learning \[[46](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_046), [47](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_047)\]. AI has demonstrated effectiveness in distinguishing between critical and noncritical cases.

ML methodologies can be employed to implement AI systems with the objective of recognizing fractures in bones in X-ray images. AI-enabled models can automatically detect bone fractures. The application of neural networks to trained datasets yields accurate outcomes when analyzing X-ray images. In addition, the discipline of medical science derives advantages from the utilization of AI, specifically DL methodologies like neural networks, which improve the presentation of imaging data. The convolutional neural network (CNN) is crucial in developing the architecture for bone fracture detection. It provides both accuracy and efficiency in producing results.

### 2.5.1 Data preprocessing

Nonmedical pictures may be utilized for pre-training the dataset, facilitating the automated identification of fractures on plain radiographs. According to researchers, preprocessing techniques are employed on fractured images within a dataset to increase desired data features and suppress undesired ones \[[48](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_048)\]. Preparing data for use in later learning models requires a large time commitment. The objective is to process data to enable analysis and computations. Overfitting may result in complications in data retrieval and selection \[[49](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_049)\]. Overfitting happens when noise in the data is incorporated during the learning process, leading to negative impacts on the outcomes. The first dataset is generated in order to facilitate the learning process, requiring an enormous amount of effort. Subsequently, a meticulous oversight of the labelling process is required. The data is classified into two categories: fracture and no fracture. Fracture identification is confirmed by an experienced surgeon with over three years of expertise. A series of radiographs is acquired from a professional medical facility and analyzed under the supervision of an experienced orthopedic surgeon.

At present, there is a diverse range of visualization tools, which are readily available for the practical application of neural networks. R Studio is a suitable platform for implementing deep neural networks. R studio offers several packages for DL, including nnet, neuralnet, deepnet, and rnet.

### 2.5.2 Bone fracture

Fractures can be classified using various methods. Traumatic fractures result from different types of trauma, including road accidents, violence, train accidents, as well as falls. The pathological fractures are a result of bone weakening caused by underlying diseases such as bony metastasis (cancer spread to the bone) or osteoporosis (increased bone porosity). Periprosthetic fractures are fractures that occur at sites of mechanical vulnerability as a result of ongoing stress on various components of an implanted device within the bone.

Fractures in children and teenagers may exhibit incomplete characteristics as a result of the inherent flexibility and reduced brittleness of their skeletal structure. When one tension cortex fails, these fractures are referred to as greenstick fractures. Torus or buckle fractures occur when the compression cortex buckles. The bone in pediatric patients has the ability to experience damage without showing a noticeable fracture line.

The classification approach encompasses the evaluation of multiple parameters, including but not limited to the structure of fractures, dislocation of fragments, the existence of dislocation of the joint or subluxation, and the number of pieces. The list below illustrates the typical classifications and associated comparisons of fractures in various aspects:

*   Stable and closed fracture: A fracture is characterized by the alignment of the broken bone ends in close proximity with minimal displacement.
    
*   Open or compound fracture: An open fracture develops when a bone extends through the dermis. The visibility of the bone within the wound is inconsistent.
    
*   Transverse fracture: If the line of the fracture is horizontal, then this type of fracture is known as transverse fracture.
    
*   Oblique fracture: This is a type of fracture which can be seen from a variety of different angles.
    
*   Comminuted fracture: This particular fracture is characterized by the fragmentation of the bone into several fragments, exceeding the count of two.
    
*   Greenstick fracture: When bone is split into fragments but not totally, this condition is referred to as incomplete bone fragmentation. In the case of youngsters, it is possible that this will occur.
    
*   Spiral fracture: This particular fracture is characterized by a progressive deterioration of the crack, resulting in a spiral pattern that extends along the bone.
    

A noticeable abnormality can be easily identified by individuals, particularly if it is significant, even without the aid of medical imaging. However, X-rays are necessary to precisely diagnose a fracture \[[50](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_050)\]. To ensure a thorough depiction of fractures, it should encompass the following details: bone nomenclature, precise anatomical location of the fractured region, fracture line sequence, the existence of compression, dislocation of the fragments of the fracture, type, and extent of displacement, previously existing pathological conditions, and any related joint pathology, such as dislocation or subluxation.

### 2.5.3 Computer vision for fracture recognition

Computer vision is a specialist field within DL that involves the ability of machines to understand and analyze images or videos. The application of this technology shows potential in improving diagnostic decision-making in fracture care. CNNs have demonstrated efficacy in the realm of computer vision issues \[[51](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_051)\]. The employment of pre-trained CNNs facilitates the transfer of information to a distinct fracture recognition task, hence diminishing the necessity for further computationally intensive training. The results demonstrate that computer vision is equivalent to, and in many instances, exceeds human capability in identifying particular categories of prevalent fractures. Proximal humerus fractures, which can have variable presentations leading to misdiagnosis, were effectively classified using a CNN. The CNN exhibited a higher level of performance in comparison to general physicians and general orthopedic surgeons while obtaining comparable results to specialized surgeons. The CNN was trained using a dataset including about 2,000 radiographs, which were categorized according to the Neer classification scheme \[[52](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_052)\]. Furthermore, it is essential to acknowledge that there is a restricted numbers of research that has been published that showcases the capacity of AI to effectively detect fractures on standard radiographs acquired in the emergency room, namely for individuals with wrist, hand, and ankle injuries. These studies have shown an accuracy rate of no less than 83% \[[5](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_005), [53](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_053), [54](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_054), [55](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_055)\]. The primary focus of these investigations focused on the incorporation of easily identified fractures.

Subtle and occult fractures pose greater challenges compared to easily detectable fractures. In cases where there is a clinical suspicion of a scaphoid fracture, it is essential to acknowledge that scaphoid fractures may prove challenging to detect on radiographs and are frequently missed by nonspecialists \[[56](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_056)\]. Certain scaphoid fractures, known as radiographically occult fractures, are undetectable on radiographs even by specialists. Computer vision did not surpass human observers in accurately identifying true fractures among suspected fractures, even when the suspected fractures were not visible on radiographs. The utilization of Gartner’s fracture recognition technology employing CNN has attracted considerable interest and financial support from several start-up companies, leading to significant expenditures in the billions of dollars. At present, it is apparent that the identification of occult fracture of the scaphoid poses challenges for both skilled surgeons and specialized CNNs \[[57](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_057)\].

A major challenge encountered in the supervised learning of CNN for musculoskeletal computer vision of occult fractures is the necessity for a considerable number of instances to effectively train the algorithm. Moreover, the reliability of the reference standard (CT, MRI, or follow-up radiographs) employed for training purposes is also subject to question. At present, computer vision has a significant sensitivity to the errors of occult scaphoid fractures, similar to that observed among orthopedic surgeons and radiologists. Computer vision has exhibited enhanced efficacy in comparison to specialized professionals in the domain of cancer screening, namely in the identification of early-stage cancers that are often missed by human experts, particularly those in different areas of specialty \[[58](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_058), [59](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_059)\]. The objective of computer vision in the field of orthopedics is to get prompt and precise diagnosis and categorization, with the ultimate goal of improving treatment results. At present, orthopedic surgeons exhibit a level of precision that is equivalent to that of AI. The initial computer vision application in orthopedics, known as OsteoDetect, fails to achieve superior accuracy compared to that of specialized professionals in the identification and diagnosis of distal radius fractures \[[60](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_060)\].

## 2.6 Different techniques of AI in bone fracture detection

### 2.6.1 Primary machine learning-based algorithm

The earliest methods employed focused specifically on the NSA (neck-shaft angle) as the principal parameter for identifying fractures. The fracture recognition in an image can be determined by evaluating the value of NSA. If the NSA value is below 116¡, it indicates the presence of a fracture. A single feature alone lacks sufficient capability to accurately identify fractures, resulting in an error rate of approximately 8%. The work presented a novel approach to identifying diseases through the utilization of classification methods. The technique involves analyzing the texture of bony trabecular patterns in femur X-rays.

The extraction of characteristics is essential for the use of basic ML techniques in fracture identification. Researchers employed a mixture of intensity gradient direction analysis, Gabor filters, and Markov random field methods to extract features from femur X-rays \[[61](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_061), [62](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_062)\]. In their study, researchers utilized Gabor filters as the sole method for feature extraction in the context of fracture identification, in conjunction with classifiers \[[63](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_063)\]. Certain researchers employ a technique known as gradient-based transformation with homotopic functions. According to researchers, regression trees and SVM are extensively utilized in several sectors, including but not limited to medical diagnosis and weather prediction \[[64](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_064), [65](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_065)\].

### 2.6.2 Ensemble-based classification system

Ensemble approaches encompass the combination of numerous algorithms inside a unified predictive framework, with the objective of augmenting the accuracy of predictions. The integration of several learning models contributes to the improvement of outcomes. Therefore, ensemble methods receive preference above other techniques. The use of an ensemble-based classification system entails the integration of multiple classifiers to provide a unified result by merging their individual outputs \[[66](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_066)\]. The ensemble methodology can be categorized into two approaches. The initial technique involves applying diverse learning methods, as compared to alternative approaches that employ a uniform learning strategy across several training sets. The classifier system employs a limited amount of foundational classifiers, which are aggregated to generate a more precise outcome \[[67](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_067)\].

Ensemble classifiers use selection procedures in order to determine the highest effective subset of classifiers. There exist two primary methodologies for the process of selection: static and dynamic. The static technique involves selecting the most effective classifier based on its performance on a validation set. There are two specific ways to put a dynamic approach into action: dynamic classifier selection and dynamic group selection. Dynamic classifier selection, or DCS, is the careful choice of base classifiers to put together an ensemble. Dynamic ensemble selection is the process of finding the best mix of ensembles that can make accurate predictions \[[68](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_068)\]. Ensemble methods, such as bagging and boosting, have been used to find fractures in medical science \[[69](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_069)\].

To find osteoporotic fractures, researchers used ensemble-based learning techniques like boosting, bagging, and random subspaces \[[70](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_070)\]. Different models are made with their own sets of traits that are used for classifying them into groups. The study by researchers used 10-fold cross-validation, an ensemble technique, for the detection of bone fractures \[[71](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_071)\]. Researchers typically employ feature selection as a preliminary step before implementing ML techniques. One advantage is that it helps to avoid unnecessary additional bone testing.

### 2.6.3 Deep learning

DL is a specific subfield of AI, defined by the integration of layers of artificial neurons. Each layer consists of many units, with each unit serving as a simplified representation of a neuronal cell, guided by its anatomical structure in the human brain \[[72](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_072)\]. Recent research has demonstrated that DL algorithms have attained performance levels that are comparable to, or even beyond, human capabilities in some tasks \[[73](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_073), [74](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_074)\]. The emergence of DL has brought about a significant transformation in the domain of information technology, as it has facilitated the creation of data-centric solutions of a vast magnitude. Consequently, this has effectively addressed problems that were previously difficult and time-intensive.

DL has experienced significant growth in recent years \[[75](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_075)\]. Several recent researches have provided evidence that DL has the capability to accomplish advanced interpretation that is on par with medical professionals \[[76](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_076)\]. Numerous investigations have employed DL methodologies in radiographic imaging to detect fractures within the area of orthopedic traumatology \[[55](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_055)\]. The current research on the utilization of DL-based methods for fracture analysis in CT scans remains restricted.

In light of the advancements of DL in computer vision, the medical imaging operations have likewise incorporated DL methodologies to tackle its own challenges. These encompass tasks including medical image classification, medical picture segmentation, and noise reduction \[[77](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_077)\]. DL algorithms are highly abstract, which eliminates the need to modify the methodology when transitioning from one field to another. Furthermore, the use of transfer learning in DL algorithms allows them to leverage prior achievements, even when the model was employed to address a distinct problem \[[78](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_078)\].

The fundamental DL layer comprises a collection of neurons that partially mimic the functioning of a biological neuron. Every individual neuron within the layer is allocated a weight, represented as “*w*,” for every input connection it receives. In addition, each neuron is allocated a bias value, represented as “*b*.” The weight variable *w* signifies the magnitude of the connection, whilst the bias value *b* permits the modification of the function that activates in accordance with the weighted sum of inputs, hence establishing the threshold at which the activation function is activated. The weight *w* serves an essential function in determining the magnitude of influence exerted by each input on the output of the neuron. On the other hand, the bias *b* facilitates the model in efficiently aligning with the provided data. An activation function, denoted as *g*, is employed on the neuron’s output *z* to introduce nonlinearity into the decision-making process and generate the neuron’s output.

Automated DL algorithms have the potential to aid in or even substitute the manual procedure of identifying and categorizing fracture on radiographs as well as CT images. These systems have improved accuracy, sensitivity, and specificity. By utilizing a large dataset of thousands of images, one can effectively tackle multiple challenges using DL techniques. The models VGG16, Inception V3, and Xception have been used for image classification tasks, such as detecting fractures and distinguishing between different types of fractures \[[79](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_079)\]. Several models, such as ResNet \[[80](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_080)\], U-net \[[81](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_081)\], Mask-RCNN \[[82](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_082)\], and Faster-RCNN \[[83](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_083)\], can be trained to detect and segment fractures using bounding box annotations or labels.

Deep neural networks get accurate results by combining forecasts from many training models \[[84](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_084)\]. In addition to better scalability and data handling flexibility, DL models allow for the assignment of different weights to distinct groups of data for each iteration. This makes different predictions possible.

The adaptive differential parameter optimization method uses computer programs called ANNs to figure out if someone has broken a bone. The technique uses a mechanism for identifying fractured lines and distinguishing them from nonfractured ones \[[85](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_085)\]. The program correctly tells whether X-ray pictures show fractures or not by using an ANN model for classification and a probabilistic Hough transform method to prepare the data. The result was 74.4% accuracy and an AUC score of 0.8149.

To identify wrist bone fractures, the researchers in this study used a deep CNN model \[[53](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_053)\]. A variety of bodily parts are depicted in the original collection’s 135,845 X-ray images. From the dataset provided, a subset of 34,990 X-ray pictures depicting the wrist bones was chosen. The first of two test datasets was constructed using 3,500 randomly selected X-rays of the wrist bones from a bigger pool of 34,990 X-rays. A total of 140 X-ray pictures made up the second collection of data. Two distinct parts make up the training procedure for the model. The model is pre-trained in the first step, and then fine-tuned to better detect wrist fractures in the second step. When applied to separate datasets, the AUC curves generated for the performance evaluation had values of around 0.967 and 0.975. Furthermore, the test has a demonstrated sensitivity of 93.9%, suggesting it might consistently detect positive cases. The study states that the test has a specificity of 94.5%, which shows that it can correctly identify false negatives.

Researchers analyzed a dataset of about 53,000 pelvic X-ray images using DenseNet, a neural network design \[[86](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_086)\]. A 172-layer CNN with 12 features/units per layer was also employed in tandem with the labeling procedure. A model with an accuracy level of 95% to more than 99% was used to create the ROC curve.

One method for detecting fractures in X-ray images is by using transfer learning, a type of deep CNN \[[5](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_005)\]. An initial dataset consisting of 11,112 photos was used to train the program. There were 695 pictures in the fracture category and 694 images in the nonfracture category in this dataset. An AUC graph was drawn as part of the performance evaluation process, which estimated a value of 0.954. This figure is obtained from the calculation of a sensitivity of 0.9 and a specificity of 0.88.

According to researchers, the integration of ANN has demonstrated efficacy in the detection of proximal humerus fractures in anteroposterior (AP) X-rays \[[87](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_087)\]. A dataset consisting of 1,891 images was used for training purposes, employing a CNN. In the data preprocessing phase, manual cropping is carried out on images to attain square dimensions. The photos are then downsized to a dimension of 256 × 256 pixels. Subsequently, the data is partitioned into 10 distinct subsets, wherein one subset is designated as primary dataset, and the remaining subsets are allocated for the purpose of training the deep CNN model. The model has a considerable degree of precision, achieving a 95% accuracy rate. The ROC curve exhibits a remarkable AUC value of 0.996, which signifies a high level of classification capability. The model demonstrates a sensitivity of 0.99, signifying a high accuracy in identifying positive cases, and a specificity of 0.97, reflecting a robust capacity to accurately detect negative situations.

Researchers apply DL techniques for efficient bone fracture detection \[[88](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_088)\]. They aim to categorize X-ray images as depicting fractured or nonfractured bones using a neural network consisting of 1,024 neurons. The categorization task will be conducted on X-ray images of several anatomical regions. The technique used a sample size of 30 participants who received training and was subsequently applied to a larger group of 70 persons. Two preprocessing techniques, namely Haar wavelet transform and SIFT, are commonly employed. The Scale-Invariant Feature Transform (SIFT) is a computational method that includes several phases, involving the extraction of unique features and the assessment of their orientations. The DL approach employing neural networks demonstrates a level of accuracy close to 94%.

Researchers use a deep neural network to precisely identify hip fractures in X-ray images \[[89](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_089)\]. The deep CNN undergoes training using a diverse range of datasets, commencing with an initial dataset including 25,505 limb X-rays. The dataset comprises X-ray images of ankles, elbows, feet, and wrists, and has undergone pre-training. The dataset comprises 3,605 X-ray images illustrating frontal pelvic views. Additionally, a set of 100 pelvic X-rays was acquired for future study. The dataset undergoes preprocessing, wherein all photos are resized to dimensions of 512 × 512 pixels. Researchers employed Densenet for the purpose of modeling the fracture identification system \[[90](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_090)\]. The accuracy and AUC values vary across datasets. During the early phase of pre-training, the achieved accuracy stands at 99.5%. Upon direct application to hip fracture images, the system demonstrated a precision rate of 90% and an AUC value of 0.98. Also, an investigation with four orthopedic surgeons and two radiologists yielded an average accuracy rate of 87.7% with a sensitivity of 98%.

A medical condition can be more accurately diagnosed with the use of computer technology, which is known as computer-aided detection, or CAD. By leveraging computational capabilities, CAD helps healthcare workers improve the accuracy of patient diagnoses and streamline the decision-making process. A wide range of patient care activities makes use of CAD systems, including lesion identification and characterization, cancer stage determination, treatment plan creation, therapeutic response assessment, and recurrence and prognosis prediction \[[91](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_091)\]. Initial cancer diagnoses use the CAD system to evaluate medical imaging, such as X-rays, CT scans, mammograms, or MRI pictures. Although the CAD results are useful as a starting point for additional research in this area, they should not be taken as final proof. CAD techniques have been developed to aid in the identification and diagnosis of various diseases and ailments, including cancers of the breast, arteries, colon, lungs, congenital heart, brain, and bone \[[92](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_092)\].

Using conventional AP shoulder radiographs, the studies aimed to determine how well DL methods detected and classified proximal humerus fractures \[[93](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_093)\]. Researchers compared the CNN network’s findings to those of orthopedic surgeons, radiologists, and general practitioners. Over 1,900 AP radiographs without enhancements made up the dataset. After starting with an existing ResNet-152 model, the researchers used their own datasets of proximal humerus fractures to further improve it. It was determined that CNN performed adequately when it came to differentiating between normal shoulders and proximal humerus fractures. Another area where simple AP shoulder radiographs performed well was in fracture type categorization. When compared to general physicians and orthopedic surgeons, the CNN performed better; but, when compared to orthopedic surgeons who specialize in shoulder disorders, it performed similarly. According to the research, plain radiographs have the ability to be used for accurate automated diagnosis and classification of proximal humerus fractures and other orthopedic disorders.

In order to determine how well DL algorithms detect osteoporotic vertebral fractures (OVFs) from CT scans, researchers performed a retrospective analysis \[[94](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_094)\]. Using a DNN framework, a machine learning approach was created to detect OVFs in CT scans without human intervention. One part of the OVF detection system used CNNs to extract features, and the other part used RNNs for combining and diagnosing features. A deep residual network (ResNet) was employed by the researchers to process and extract features from CT scans. A total of 1,168 CT scans made up the training dataset, with an additional 135 and 129 CT scans in the validation and test sets, respectively. When tested on a separate dataset, the accuracy and F1 score of the proposed method were found to be identical to those of experienced radiologists. The possible benefits of implementing this automated detection system include a reduction in the time and manual workload required by radiologists for screening OVFs. Moreover, it has been proposed that this methodology may reduce the incidence of false-negative errors in the classification of asymptomatic early-stage vertebral fractures.

## 2.7 Value of deep learning in radiology/orthopedic traumatology

The researchers have explained the potential benefits of integrating DL algorithms into routine radiology practice, particularly in tasks pertaining to fracture diagnosis and characterization ([Figure 2.5](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_fig_005)). The use of DL methodologies has potential in augmenting the efficacy and accuracy of diagnostic tests within the field of radiology, primarily through a reduction of the work load placed upon human radiologists. Furthermore, it should be noted that DL systems face comparable issues as those faced in human-based diagnosis, including inter- and intra-observer variability. When employed in academic research settings, DL has exhibited the capability to attain equivalent or even superior performance to human experts in the identification and categorization of fractures on plain radiographs and CT scans.

![Figure 2.5: Workflow in fracture identification with the help of deep learning. (Created with BioRender.com)](graphic/converted/b_9783111549729-002_fig_005.png)

Figure 2.5: Workflow in fracture identification with the help of deep learning. (Created with BioRender.com)

### 2.7.1 Combining deep learning with a radiomics approach

Radiomics is a process used to extract a significant number of specific quantitative features from medical images that are too detailed for the human visual system to understand. DL involves the use of the complete image for the aim of learning, whereas radiomics concentrates exclusively on the region of interest (ROI) that is linked to a particular disease. From our perspective, it may be argued that DL and radiomics present mutually beneficial imaging biomarkers. Moreover, incorporating radiomics features in models can be advantageous due to their stability with smaller datasets, serving as a safeguard against potential overfitting of deep learning networks.

## 2.8 Role of AI in bone cancer

AI denotes the capacity of a machine to do activities at a level equivalent to human capabilities. This is accomplished by employing distinctive data inputs to produce outputs with significant additional value \[[95](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_095)\]. Recent advancements in medical imaging, along with the accessibility of extensive imaging and report data, have stimulated global interest in the application of AI approaches for medical imaging \[[96](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_096)\]. The primary justification for the creation and use of AI technologies was to aid clinicians, particularly radiologists, in identifying and characterizing lesions, hence enhancing efficiency and detection precision while minimizing diagnostic errors \[[97](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_097)\]. Recent advancements in computing and AI have led to the exploration of several applications, including lesion characterization, automated segmentation, and decision support planning, such as forecasting phenotypes and outcomes \[[98](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_098)\].

ML constitutes a subset of AI wherein models are trained to anticipate outcomes using datasets having established true foundations, from which the machine acquires knowledge. The integrated AI model can subsequently use its acquired expertise to forecast results in previously unexamined datasets \[[89](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_089)\]. Radionics is an emerging branch of ML that entails transforming the information contained in medical images into measurable and quantifiable data \[[99](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_099)\]. The acquired information can subsequently assist radiologists and doctors in evaluating benign and malignant lesions by analyzing and employing further details beyond visual interpretation (discernible by the human eye) \[[100](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_100), [101](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_101)\]. The integration of radiomics with supplementary clinical and qualitative imaging data can assist clinical decision-making by improving lesion detection, categorization, prediction, and treatment response evaluations \[[102](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_102)\]. The standard workflow for developing a radiomics model comprises the following steps: image acquisition, data selection (image input), segmentation, extraction of image features from the designated ROIs, exploratory investigation with feature selection, and modeling \[[103](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_103)\] ([Figure 2.6](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_fig_006)). The models must thereafter undergo validation using test sets, ideally incorporating both internal and external data, to assess their performance \[[104](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_104)\].

![Figure 2.6: An overview of the main steps and structure of radiomics, including acquiring images, processing them (including segmentation), extracting features from the ROIs, selecting features, doing exploratory analysis, and finally, modeling. (Created with BioRender.com)](graphic/converted/b_9783111549729-002_fig_006.png)

Figure 2.6: An overview of the main steps and structure of radiomics, including acquiring images, processing them (including segmentation), extracting features from the ROIs, selecting features, doing exploratory analysis, and finally, modeling. (Created with BioRender.com)

Imaging feature analysis based on radiomics, and CNNs are the two most used machine learning approaches ([Figure 2.7](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_fig_007)). One approach to feature-based radiomics is to collect a number of manually-created features; these can then form a training set for AI-based image categorization \[[105](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_105)\]. Whereas CNNs make advantage of DL to generate valuable imaging features through pattern learning, direct data classification from input images \[[106](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_106)\], and transformation of these features into outputs \[[107](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_107), [108](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_108)\]. The end result is an improvement in diagnostic pattern and feature recognition and processing capabilities compared to human readers; these capabilities are subsequently used to a range of tasks, such as lesion identification, characterization, and prognostic information provision \[[109](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_109)\].

![Figure 2.7: A schematic representation depicting the distinction between the prevailing methodologies in ML, namely feature-based radiomics and DL, with respect to their application in lesion classification. (Created with BioRender.com)](graphic/converted/b_9783111549729-002_fig_007.png)

Figure 2.7: A schematic representation depicting the distinction between the prevailing methodologies in ML, namely feature-based radiomics and DL, with respect to their application in lesion classification. (Created with BioRender.com)

### 2.8.1 AI in bone cancer detection

Timely detection and accurate classification are essential for ensuring optimal treatment of bone tumors. Early detection is essential for the potential of radical surgical resection in primary malignancies \[[110](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_110), [111](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_111)\]. Several therapies, including radiation, surgical augmentation, or protective weight-bearing, may be required to avoid fractures close to osteolytic tumors \[[112](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_112), [113](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_113)\]. When screening for bone tumors, plain radiographs are commonly used. Reports indicate a high rate of misdiagnosis in bone tumors due to their varied morphologies and ambiguous features \[[95](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_095), [114](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_114)\]. Advanced imaging modalities, including CT, bone scan, MRI, and positron emission tomography (PET), exhibit greater sensitivity in the detection of bone tumors. Nevertheless, the regular utilization of these methods is linked to significant expenses and time commitments.

AI advancements are contributing to the progress in analysis of data for healthcare \[[96](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_096), [97](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_097)\]. DL is an approach to neural networks that attempts to simulate brain activity. It possesses the ability to address intricate problems that surpass the capabilities of rudimentary AI. CNN models have demonstrated exceptional efficacy in interpreting intricate patterns in medical imaging. DL has attained proficiency in evaluating two-dimensional medical images comparable to those of an average human specialist in the field \[[98](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_098)\]. Numerous studies indicate that plain radiography, ultrasonography, CT, MRI, microscope, and endoscopy are effective diagnostic modalities for illness identification and categorization \[[89](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_089), [99](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_099), [100](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_100)\]. AI technology can potentially identify bone tumors on plain radiographs. Integrating an AI-driven classification system in clinical practice may significantly decrease time, expenses, and human errors.

## 2.9 Methods for the detection of cancer

The detecting technique consists of three distinct processes to increase the accuracy of the results. The stage of picture enhancement holds significant importance within the framework of an image processing system. During this stage, the image undergoes noise reduction and removal of any interruptions or distortions. Three methods are employed for this objective: The Gabor filter is a linear filter commonly employed for texture analysis in image processing. An automated method is employed to improve the color and contrast of an image. However, the use of the fast Fourier transform (FFT) is commonly observed in the process of image transformation, where it separates the image into its constituent sine and cosine components. It is worth noting that the FFT may not produce optimal outcomes when employed for image segmentation.

### 2.9.1 Steps of cancer diagnosis in medical imaging

The process of diagnosing cancer based on medical pictures encompasses multiple stages, which include collecting images, the preprocessing phase, segmentation, feature identification, and classification.

#### 2.9.1.1 Image acquisition methods

The initial stage entails acquiring images by a range of techniques, including X-ray, computed tomography (CT), ultrasound, magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), nuclear medicine imaging, microscope, thermography, and mammography.

#### 2.9.1.2 Preprocessing

Preprocessing involves modifications made to quantity images at a lower degree of abstraction, taking place throughout both the input and output stages \[[101](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_101)\]. Various picture operations, including enhancement of images, image normalization, image sharpness, intensity modification, and noise removal, are executed with the purpose of extracting valuable information that facilitates the process of diagnosis. The technique of image preprocessing serves as the initial stage in the preparation of an image for further analysis \[[102](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_102)\]. Several incorrect classifications can result from inadequate noise issue resolution. Misclassification may occur in images including both lesions and hairs. There are a number of significant methods for processing images \[[103](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_103), [104](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_104)\].

#### 2.9.1.3 Segmentation

Image segmentation is the process of dividing a digital image into smaller, more manageable pieces based on similarities in characteristics like brightness, contrast, and texture. In order to create objects of interest for additional analysis and interpretation, image segmentation extracts unique properties from images and then combines or separates them \[[105](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_105)\]. The primary goal of segmentation is to improve the picture’s interpretability and analysis capabilities by changing its visual representation. Step one of the process is the extraction of attributes \[[106](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_106)\]. Images must be segmented in order for various objects and restrictions, including lines and curves, to be located and identified. As part of the segmentation process, each pixel is assigned a label that corresponds to its common visual attributes. Segmentation algorithms are dependent on two essential characteristics of intensity levels, namely similarity and discontinuity. The main aim of using discontinuity is to divide the image into separate segments according to abrupt variations in intensity, such as the presence of lines inside the image. The assessment of similarity entails dividing the image into distinct segments that meet an established criterion. Histogram thresholding is a segmentation technique that falls under the category of property similarity.

#### 2.9.1.4 Feature detection

The current step has significant importance as it uses a range of algorithms and approaches to effectively determine and differentiate distinct segments or patterns (referred to as features) inside the given image. Binarization and masking techniques are used in the estimation of the likelihood of tumor. The binarization technique depends on the observation that typical tumor pictures have a notably greater frequency of black pixels in comparison to white pixels. The masking technique depends on the finding that malignant tumors manifest as contiguous white regions inside the pulmonary system. The presence of cancer is directly proportional to the increase in these masses \[[107](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_107)\]. Tissue features are valuable for distinguishing between normal and abnormal masses \[[108](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_108)\].

#### 2.9.1.5 Classification

Picture classification is a widely used technique within the domain of picture mining. The process of image classification entails the selection of a model from a repository of image information with the objective of predicting the class label of an unfamiliar image. The decision-making process relies on the use of classified data. Diverse classification methodologies are commonly employed for classification purposes, including supervised, unsupervised, reinforcement, and active learning techniques \[[109](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_109)\].

#### 2.9.1.6 Image visualization

AI serves a vital function in cancer imaging by performing three primary clinical tasks: tumor detection, tumor characterization, and tumor monitoring. Detection in radiography involves identifying and locating objects of interest, which is commonly referred to as computer-aided detection (CADe) \[[9](#9783111547589_003062.xhtml#b_9783111549729-002_ref_009)\].

## 2.10 Conclusion

AI-based applications possess the capability to enhance personalized care for patients by using data-driven approaches. These applications can help mitigate the influence of surgeons’ biases and enable collaborative decision-making based on patient-specific information. It is probable that AI will provide support to orthopedic surgeons rather than supplant them. The incorporation of AI technology in healthcare is expected to alleviate the burden of routine tasks, enabling doctors to allocate more time to the practice of medicine. The utilization of AI-based techniques in the domain of orthopedics, particularly in the identification of bone fractures and bone cancer, as well as during surgical procedures, has the potential to bring about significant transformative effects. This will result in improved patient care within a limited timeframe and with limited available resources. AI has the capability to address the growing need for handling monotonous and repetitive tasks that require less cognitive engagement. AI techniques for categorizing bone lesions have demonstrated generally beneficial results across various imaging modalities, exhibiting significant sensitivity, specificity, and accuracy in distinguishing between benign and malignant lesions. The implementation of these strategies has the potential to enhance the management of bone disorders.

# Chapter 3 AI for remote patient monitoring in healthcare

#### Abstract

An enormous rise in the use of artificial intelligence (AI) in healthcare is being seen. Remote patient monitoring, or RPM, is a common healthcare tool that helps doctors keep an eye on patients with long-term or short-term illnesses, who live in remote areas. It can also be used to keep an eye on old people who are getting care at home as well as hospitalized patients. The staff’s efficiency, which is influenced by their duties, is key to the reliability of manual patient monitoring systems. Standard ways of keeping an eye on a patient usually involve uncomfortable methods, which may require touching the patient’s skin to check on their health. The goal of this chapter is to give a full look at RPM systems, covering topics like how new technologies are being used, how AI is changing RPM, and the problems and new trends in AI-enhanced RPM. This chapter also talks about the advantages of RPM systems that focus on the patient, and are made possible by Internet of things sensors and wearable tech. Tech like cloud, fog, edge, and blockchain are used in these designs. AI is used in many areas of RPM, such as classifying physical exercise, keeping an eye on long-term illnesses, and checking vital signs in an emergency. This chapter shows how AI-driven RPM systems have changed healthcare tracking applications in a big way. These architectures have been shown to be good at finding early signs that a patient’s health is getting worse and at customizing the tracking of each patient’s health parameters using federated learning. Taking into account the many challenges and new trends in the field, this chapter looks at the possible future paths of AI in RPM uses.

Keywords:  Patient,  monitoring,  artificial intelligence,  risk prediction,  internet of thing,  mobile health,  

## 3.1 Introduction

In the growing area of RPM, doctors can get help in providing care across multiple hospital wards. In order to do this, wearable devices made from flexible materials are used \[[1](#9783111547589_005273.xhtml#b_9783111549729-003_ref_001)\]. New Internet of things (IoT) tactics are being used in medicine, such as telehealth apps, wearable tech, and contact-based sensors, which help this progress \[[2](#9783111547589_005273.xhtml#b_9783111549729-003_ref_002)\]. Remote patient monitoring (RPM) is often used to check vital signs and other physiological data, such as detecting movement \[[3](#9783111547589_005273.xhtml#b_9783111549729-003_ref_003), [4](#9783111547589_005273.xhtml#b_9783111549729-003_ref_004)\]. The information so collected can help doctors make decisions and plan treatments for a wide range of diseases, such as movement disorders and mental disorders \[[5](#9783111547589_005273.xhtml#b_9783111549729-003_ref_005)\].

Artificial intelligence (AI) algorithms are used to handle medical images and connect clinical data with biomarkers and symptoms. This helps individuals understand the sickness better and makes it easier to guess how it will turn out \[[6](#9783111547589_005273.xhtml#b_9783111549729-003_ref_006)\]. Clinicians are looking into how AI could be used to improve healthcare service. They are focusing on practical issues like figuring out how likely it is that a patient will get sick, for keeping track of them all the time, and for using AI to stop diseases from getting worse \[[7](#9783111547589_005273.xhtml#b_9783111549729-003_ref_007)\]. AI is a very important part of medical study because it speeds up genome sequencing and helps find new medicines and treatments. This technology makes it possible to collect and analyze large amounts of complex data that could not be seen or accessed before. A type of AI called machine learning uses special methods to help doctors assess large amounts of data \[[8](#9783111547589_005273.xhtml#b_9783111549729-003_ref_008)\]. In order to identify early health decline and organize a patient’s movements or activities, they can help with evaluations. AI algorithms can look through huge datasets to find and understand complex trends, which makes it easier to make decisions \[[9](#9783111547589_005273.xhtml#b_9783111549729-003_ref_009)\]. Better computer power has made it possible to make artificial neural networks (ANNs) and deep learning algorithms that are more reliable, and can quickly process and optimize large datasets \[[10](#9783111547589_005273.xhtml#b_9783111549729-003_ref_010)\]. The incorporation of an IoT model with a centralized control unit and interaction allows for the automation of numerous routine tasks. This strategy has the capacity to improve patient safety and reduce human errors \[[11](#9783111547589_005273.xhtml#b_9783111549729-003_ref_011)\].

RPM has been implemented historically to supervise patients in rural areas through the utilization of telehealth technology. It has also been employed to monitor chronically unwell and elderly individuals at home by utilizing wearable devices or sensors. The nonintrusive characteristics of RPM render it advantageous for hospitals, particularly for postsurgical patients and those in intensive care unit (ICU), who can gain from the utilization of wireless body sensors. The monitoring systems can be improved by incorporating noninvasive digital technologies that enable the tracking of patients’ daily activities. Machine learning (ML) and AI can aid medical professionals in depicting patients’ health status by interpreting vital signs and detecting their actions ([Figure 3.1](#9783111547589_005273.xhtml#b_9783111549729-003_fig_001)). These applications can offer data for the purpose of diagnosing and predicting the health status of patients as well as in clinical decision-making.

![Figure 3.1: Architectures for remote patient monitoring empowered by artificial intelligence.](graphic/converted/b_9783111549729-003_fig_001.png)

Figure 3.1: Architectures for remote patient monitoring empowered by artificial intelligence.

## 3.2 Introduction to remote patient monitoring

Chronic illnesses, including cancer, heart disease, diabetes, asthma, and mental disorders, have already overtaken infectious diseases as the primary causes of mortality and morbidity worldwide \[[12](#9783111547589_005273.xhtml#b_9783111549729-003_ref_012), [13](#9783111547589_005273.xhtml#b_9783111549729-003_ref_013)\]. The rising frequency of numerous chronic illnesses is anticipated as a result of both the aging people and the acceptance of unhealthy lifestyles. Chronic conditions pose a significant challenge because to the absence of readily available therapeutic alternatives. People having chronic conditions need to continually handle their illness to avoid the occurrence of acute symptoms. This involves regular monitoring of diet, workouts, and medicine. There is an increasing need for the monitoring of patients’ solutions to aid in the management of chronic diseases and improve self-care \[[14](#9783111547589_005273.xhtml#b_9783111549729-003_ref_014), [15](#9783111547589_005273.xhtml#b_9783111549729-003_ref_015)\].

RPM represents an increasing domain within healthcare that utilizes information technology and telecommunications tools to effectively manage and diagnose illnesses, ultimately providing treatment and care. This system utilizes a range of tools to collect medical information from individuals in their own homes or during their daily activities.
