# Chapter 6 Integration of AI in brain tumor surgery

#### Abstract

The surgery for brain tumors could get better and more effective with the help of artificial intelligence (AI). This chapter looks at the present and future effects of AI on brain tumor patients undergoing procedures. It focuses on key areas such as diagnosis, surgical planning, help during surgery, and prognostic predictions. Recent advances in computer vision (CV) and deep learning have made it much easier to understand the events during surgery by making it easier to analyze intraoperative video. Deep learning, on the other hand, does challenging tasks with many-layered neural networks. This makes it easier to find things in surgical images. Because of this growth, it’s easier to find different surgical steps and tools, which makes the whole process more precise. As AI technologies get better, they promise to make many parts of medical practice better. For example, they will make it easier to make accurate diagnoses, run surgeries more quickly, care for patients before and after surgery, and make sure that strict safety rules are followed. Combining CV, machine learning, and robots is one way that AI could make a big difference in neurosurgery. This could help patients have better results and speed up the surgery process. This chapter talks about how AI has changed surgery for brain tumors and how it can help neurosurgeons come up with new ideas and do their work better overall. Smart computers, or AI, might make it easier for doctors to do difficult procedures, especially in cardiothoracic surgery. Using AI technology, doctors can improve their skills and do more complicated surgeries with higher success rates, fewer problems, and safer patients. AI can also be a useful tool for doing in-depth study and quickly sharing information.

Keywords:  Artificial intelligence,  surgery,  brain tumor,  machine learning,  deep learning,  oncology,  

## 6.1 Introduction

The field of medicine has witnessed ongoing advancements in technology, characterized by a predominantly exponential rather than linear progression. Nevertheless, the field of medicine has never witnessed such a swift and substantial progression in technological advancements as observed in artificial intelligence (AI) technology and machine learning (ML) applications. The study of AI encompasses a wide range of subfields. However, at its core, AI is built upon fundamental principles such as: (1) analysis of substantial volumes of data, (2) identification and interpretation of patterns, (3) ability to make predictions about future events, and therefore, (4) facilitation of informed decision-making processes to enhance operational efficiencies. The primary objective of any novel technology should be to aid, enhance, and streamline the human experience. Therefore, it is imperative that AI applications in the field of medicine and surgery be carefully planned and executed with the primary objective of providing assistance to healthcare providers, rather than replacing them. As stated, the objective is to effectively utilize information that is beyond the cognitive capacities of humans. People will get better care, and doctors will find it easier to find treatments that work best for each patient. This will also make their work easier.

AI employs complex algorithms to combine different methods to analyze huge amounts of data, find trends, and predict what will happen. The study of AI is all about making and testing programs that make computers think and act like people. Many diverse actions are included in the steps, including reasoning, solving problems, naming words and things, and choosing options \[[1](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_001)\]. The healthcare field, for instance, uses AI in many different ways. A look at records is one way AI can be used to make hospitals and offices run more smoothly. Also, AI can be used to do things like watch videos during surgery. When it comes to image analysis and risk assessment before and during surgery, AI has given surgeons a plethora of technological possibilities.

Imagine working closely under the watchful eye of a very skilled surgeon who has done many successful procedures, dealt with many challenges, is very strong, and is always ready to help when needed. Since the early 2000s, AI has not been commonly used in surgery rooms. This problem mostly came from how hard it is to teach computers to understand visual information, which is a very important skill for medical workers. Utilizing ML methods to teach computers how to carry out surgical treatments is the main goal. This can be done with data streams from the operating room, which includes information about tools being present and electrosurgical energy equipment being turned on. Experts added notes to the data lines. Because it could not feel its surroundings when it did not have visual perception, AI did not have visual awareness. This meant that it had a limited idea of how things worked.

New developments in computer vision (CV) have used deep learning to make computers able to recognize pictures as well as humans \[[2](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_002)\]. Deep learning is a type of AI that uses many layers of neural networks to learn how to do complicated tasks and process data \[[3](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_003)\]. A common type of neuronal design is convolutional neural networks (CNNs). Their structure is similar to that of the human visual brain, which makes it easier to learn how to recognize things in pictures \[[4](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_004)\]. CNNs are very important because they are resilient. Normal ML software can be programmed to identify the difference between a certain groups of musical instruments. Even so, the training process can fail and is affected by changes in the visible environment. There are many things that can make it hard to see in the surgical field, such as smoke, blood, moving port sites, and the chance of camera pictures being blurry. Like people, CNNs are better at finding similarities and adapting to new scenarios.

CNNs can now correctly identify and classify pictures of surgeries. This is a big step forward in the field of CV. The surgical procedure is distinguished by a temporal sequence of events, in which the effects of previous actions are carried over to subsequent ones. Consequently, the use of video recordings is deemed more suitable for capturing the comprehensive nature of surgery, as opposed to relying solely on a limited number of images. Nevertheless, the application of image recognition systems to surgical video has been impeded by performance limits. Specifically, computers have faced challenges in comprehending temporal aspects of the procedure due to the absence of contextual information. The individuals have a functional visual cortex, although exhibited an absence of the memory component within their cognitive abilities. The previously mentioned constraint was resolved by integrating long short-term memory (LSTM) neural networks, which function as an equivalent to a “memory cortex” and contribute to augmenting overall effectiveness \[[5](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_005)\].

The combination of CNNs and LSTM models allows computers to develop visual and temporal learning capacities, hence enhancing their understanding of surgical operations.

## 6.2 General applications of AI in surgery

### 6.2.1 Automated identification of surgical phases

The research on AI in surgery has placed significant emphasis on instructing algorithms to comprehend operating events. In a manner akin to the acquisition of knowledge by a medical student regarding the operational phase, researchers initially imparted training to algorithms with the objective of enabling them to autonomously discern the specific phase occurring at a certain time point. Before the advent of the CV revolution, computers faced challenges in effectively processing video data. As a result, ML techniques were employed to acquire knowledge from data streams that were more compatible with computer systems. The data streams in question generally comprised of human-annotated binary data pertaining to the presence or absence of surgical equipment within a certain time frame, together with the activation timings of electrosurgical energy equipment. Various ML techniques, such as hidden Markov models (HMMs), random forests, and support vector machines, have been employed in the domain of laparoscopic surgery. This surgical procedure involves a predictable operative phase due to the utilization of limited equipment. Notably, these ML techniques have demonstrated successful applications in cholecystectomy and hysterectomy procedures \[[6](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_006), [7](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_007), [8](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_008), [9](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_009)\]. Certain initial achievements also involved the utilization of fundamental video processing techniques in order to identify visually distinguishable occurrences, such as the process of cleaning extracorporeal cameras \[[10](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_010)\].

Before the widespread adoption of CNNs, the task of processing video data posed significant challenges for traditional ML techniques. In order to provide an illustrative example, it is important to acknowledge that a solitary minute of high-resolution surgical video encompasses a much greater amount of data, approximately 25 times more, compared to a high-resolution computed tomography scan \[[11](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_011)\]. In order to overcome this challenge, first attempts in surgical video analysis employed strategies that involved reducing the information content of a picture prior to learning from this decreased data. The study addressed the identification of stages involved in sleeve gastrectomy, cholecystectomy, and ophthalmologic procedures \[[12](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_012), [13](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_013), [14](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_014)\]. Most CV methods at the time could not be used on a larger scale because they needed a lot of manual work to get the best results for a certain set of videos.

It is possible to make accurate and portable AI tools for identifying and classifying surgical processes by using CNNs. In 2016, researchers published a groundbreaking study that introduced EndoNet, a CNN which is very good at correctly identifying the different stages of laparoscopic cholecystectomy. The experts have also made Cholec80, the first reference dataset, available to everyone \[[15](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_015)\]. Researchers found that CNN was not able to consistently identify objects because it did not understand temporal context well enough. Using a common temporal ML modeling method, especially a HMM, they improved CNN’s performance by making object recognition much more consistent. In the past, experts have improved standard CNNs by adding HMMs or other methods, like light gradient boosting machines \[[16](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_016), [17](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_017)\]. Incorporating LSTM models into robots to give them temporal memory greatly improved their ability to recognize and understand surgery events based on prior information. The technique mentioned above has been extensively utilized in various surgical procedures, such as laparoscopic surgeries (e.g., sleeve gastrectomy, cholecystectomy, and colectomy), as well as ophthalmic and endoscopic operations like peroral endoscopic myotomy \[[18](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_018)\], in order to achieve accurate phase recognition. The algorithms’ ability to accurately detect phases ranges from 85% to 90%, which may be considered very low at first view. However, it is important to note that this level of accuracy is comparable to the agreement observed among different surgeons when they annotate the same photos \[[19](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_019)\].

In light of the successful attainment of dependable and precise phase recognition, current advancements have primarily concentrated on making incremental enhancements to the CNN/LSTM framework in order to obtain marginal accuracy increases for smaller datasets (1%e5%). Certain approaches incorporate supplementary input streams, such as tool recognition, in order to refine the process of phase recognition \[[20](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_020)\]. Researchers have made advancements in enhancing the memory component of neural networks by incorporating current developments that go beyond the LSTM architecture. These improvements aim to enhance the computer’s ability to retain information about past occurrences, leading to more precise identifications \[[21](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_021)\]. One final set of recent advancements has been dedicated to addressing a forthcoming issue: the accessibility of data. Ideally, it would be advantageous to provide extensive training to these algorithms by utilizing a substantial dataset consisting of thousands of videos. A significant number of videos remain unrecorded at present. Furthermore, even if these videos were available, surgeons would still be required to view and annotate them in order to create the necessary annotations for the algorithms to learn from. In order to improve this situation, scholars have implemented a more effective training approach known as “pre-training.” This involves providing algorithms with unlabeled surgical video data, enabling them to independently identify and comprehend recurring patterns inside surgical procedures, such as the temporal sequencing of various images or the duration of surgeries \[[22](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_022), [23](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_023)\]. Another strategy involves employing computers to engage in self-teaching through the utilization of machine-produced annotations, which are generated subsequent to their initial learning from a limited number of videos. Despite its seeming paradox, the process of machines developing through self-generated learning has been found to result in enhanced performance \[[24](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_024)\].

### 6.2.2 Automated identification of instruments

The recognition of tools by AI, like the progression of surgical phases, has experienced a gradual and incremental development. The early stages of development were significantly constrained due to the inherent challenges associated with picture identification. However, a notable achievement was made in the form of successfully tracking surgical tooltips during retinal surgery \[[25](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_025)\]. The domain experienced significant growth after the integration of deep learning techniques and CNNs. Previous investigations have proven successful detection of instruments in video frames during laparoscopic and cataract surgeries \[[26](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_026), [27](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_027)\]. Concurrently, the field made efforts to distinguish precise outlines of instruments depicted in the photos by the utilization of more sophisticated methodologies, such as the capability to monitor the tips of instruments in real-time ([Figure 6.1](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_fig_001)) \[[28](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_028), [29](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_029), [30](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_030), [31](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_031)\]. Previous research has demonstrated that the inclusion of a “memory component” resulted in enhanced performance compared to models that solely rely on visual information, such as CNNs \[[32](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_032)\]. The process of training a system to accurately identify instrument outlines is an intensive task that requires a significant amount of time and effort, often exceeding the requirements of phase training. This is because the machine acquires knowledge from manual outlines provided by a surgeon, which depict the precise placement of an instrument. Efforts aimed at addressing this issue have encompassed the utilization of pre-training approaches. These methods involve initially training the algorithm to autonomously learn and identify pertinent visual cues, thereby standardizing the colors of videos (akin to the concept of “white balancing”). Hence the approach has resulted in a significant reduction of 75% in the requirement for manual annotations \[[33](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_033)\]. A different approach involved training a neural network just using binary tool presence annotations, which yielded favorable outcomes in terms of tool localization and tracking \[[34](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_034)\].

![Figure 6.1: Demonstration of automated tool tracking and automated estimate of remaining surgical phase duration \[28, 29, 30, 31\].](graphic/converted/b_9783111549729-006_fig_001.jpg)

Figure 6.1: Demonstration of automated tool tracking and automated estimate of remaining surgical phase duration \[[28](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_028), [29](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_029), [30](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_030), [31](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_031)\].

## 6.3 Role of AI in brain tumor surgery

The field of AI has the potential to significantly impact the management of brain tumors. This is accomplished by incorporating various subfields, including ML, natural language processing (NLP), CV, and robotics. The amalgamation of these subfields plays a crucial role in advancing the field of neurosurgical practices by fostering the creation of cutting-edge AI applications. This section provides an analysis of the current advancements and potential clinical applications of AI in the various stages of brain tumor surgery, including pre-operative, intraoperative, and postoperative phases. The focus of this discussion is mostly on intrinsic brain tumors.

![Figure 6.2: The clinical implications of artificial intelligence (AI) in the management of brain tumors in neurosurgery include potential benefits in the preoperative, intraoperative, and postoperative phases.](graphic/converted/b_9783111549729-006_fig_002.png)

Figure 6.2: The clinical implications of artificial intelligence (AI) in the management of brain tumors in neurosurgery include potential benefits in the preoperative, intraoperative, and postoperative phases.

### 6.3.1 Preoperative phase

The anticipated impact of AI on the prognosis of individuals diagnosed with brain tumors is projected to become evident prior to their surgical treatment (refer to [Figure 6.2](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_fig_002)). Previous studies have demonstrated the beneficial effects of AI on many preoperative phases, including assessment, diagnosis, and planning \[[35](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_035), [36](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_036), [37](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_037), [38](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_038)\].

#### 6.3.1.1 Screening and diagnosis

Intracranial tumors may present with a wide array of symptoms, which might appear at different stages of the disease. Certain individuals may exhibit symptoms at an early stage of their development, whereas others may have no clinical impact regardless of their progression \[[39](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_039)\]. The variability in the degree of presentation, which is characterized by heterogeneity, may partially account for the occurrence of diagnostic delays in 30% of brain tumor patients in the United Kingdom \[[40](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_040), [41](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_041)\]. The potential for AI to enhance early detection is a subject of ongoing research and development. Recent advancements have led to the development of a ML algorithm that can effectively utilize blood test data to predict and detect the presence of brain tumors \[[42](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_042)\]. The phrase “brain tumor” comprises a range of various pathological conditions, each of which has a specific pattern on conventional blood tests. This pattern arises from the release of specific molecules unique to each tumor type within the neoplastic microenvironment. The aforementioned compounds possess the capability to pass through the blood-brain barrier and get access to the wider circulatory system \[[43](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_043), [44](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_044), [45](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_045)\]. The small alterations observed in routine blood tests make them an optimal target for ML analysis. ML models have demonstrated superior performance compared to doctors in the diagnosis of hematological diseases, as indicated in previous studies \[[46](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_046)\]. In their study, researchers employed a ML system to identify small variations in normal blood tests as a means of detecting the existence of brain tumors during clinical manifestation. The ML method identified many significant patterns within the cancer group, which encompassed an elevation in serum glucose levels and neutrophil count, and a decline in basophil and eosinophil counts. The model exhibited a sensitivity of 96% and a specificity of 74%, which aligns with the sensitivity rates observed in CT and MRI neuroimaging studies \[[47](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_047)\]. The authors of the study predicted that the model could serve as a viable screening tool for intracranial malignancy, notwithstanding its inability to reliably differentiate between primary brain tumors. In a recent publication, researchers presented findings on a detection tool that utilizes AI and employs differential scanning fluorometry of blood samples for the identification of glioblastoma in patients. The study reported a high accuracy rate of 92% for this diagnostic approach \[[48](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_048)\]. With the continuous progress of technology, it becomes increasingly plausible to utilize blood indicators for the purpose of risk stratification in brain tumor patients and to provide guidance in their management. The aforementioned discovery holds significant potential in a scientific discipline where the identification and tracking of brain tumors by blood biomarkers have proven challenging due to the presence of substantial intratumoral heterogeneity \[[49](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_049)\].

Neuroimaging, particularly magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), continues to be regarded as the gold standard for diagnosing brain tumors. According to researchers, the influence of AI can be seen even prior to the creation of radiological images. The researchers devised a ML algorithm based on NLP, which was capable of interpreting requests for MRI brain scans. Subsequently, the system selected suitable MRI brain imaging sequence to produce best diagnostically valuable images. The result highlights the potential impact of AI in optimizing the generation of radiological images. The ML methods demonstrated superior performance compared to the sequence decision made by radiologists \[[50](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_050)\]. The selection of radiological sequences is normally carried out by a radiologist; nevertheless, it is important to note that the protocoling workflow is susceptible to errors \[[51](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_051)\]. Moreover, previous studies have demonstrated that the implementation of sequence inquiries for radiographers throughout their shifts has a significant effect on the time allocation of radiologists \[[52](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_052)\] and might lead to disruptions in the process of image interpretation \[[53](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_053)\]. ML algorithms that determine sequences may have a potential function in the standardization of MRI sequence protocols, leading to the optimization of clinical application for the resulting scans \[[54](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_054)\]. Additionally, it has been observed by researchers that in the process of selecting sequences radiologists typically encounter difficulties when dealing with uncommon medical problems. However, it was observed that the ML approach exhibited exceptional performance in such circumstances, as exemplified by its effectiveness in diagnosing glioblastoma multiforme (GBM).

There is a growing recognition that radiological pictures can be considered three-dimensional data sets that can be subjected to algorithmic analysis, surpassing the interpretations made by individual clinicians \[[55](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_055)\]. The discipline of radiomics employs sophisticated computational methodologies to examine large collections of image-derived data, encompassing individual three-dimensional pixels known as “voxels,” in order to identify and describe patterns that are frequently detailed in their expression \[[56](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_056)\]. To date, radiomics has demonstrated efficacy in the detection and characterization of several cancers across different anatomical regions in the human body \[[57](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_057), [58](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_058)\]. ML algorithms have been utilized in the domain of brain tumor diagnostics for a multitude of purposes. The applications encompass the analysis of molecular expression in brain tumors \[[59](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_059), [60](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_060)\], aiding in the detection of CNS metastases \[[61](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_061), [62](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_062)\], differentiation between primary and metastatic CNS lesions \[[63](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_063)\], prognostication of brain tumor grade \[[64](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_064), [65](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_065)\], and prediction of genetic mutations \[[66](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_066), [67](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_067), [68](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_068)\], among various other uses. The outcomes have been observed in several CNS cancers, such as meningioma, glioblastoma, and CNS metastases. Moreover, it has been demonstrated that ML programs exhibit a higher level of proficiency in the detection and diagnosis of brain cancers compared to human capabilities \[[69](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_069)\].

Significant breakthroughs have been observed in the utilization of machine learning algorithms for the interpretation of medical imaging in the field of intracranial tumors within the past decade. The genetic features of gliomas, like having an isocitrate dehydrogenase (IDH) mutation or a 1p/19q mutation, are strongly linked to how quickly the disease gets worse and have been shown to affect the effectiveness of several treatment approaches \[[70](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_070)\]. In the past 10 years, ML radiomic research has focused on trying to identify these traits from imaging data so that invasive biopsies might not be needed as often. Scientists did study that showed they could make a ML system that could accurately predict the IDH1 expression status of low-grade gliomas. MRI brain scans were used in the system, which showed a high level of accuracy in just 18 min \[[71](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_071)\]. Using molecular diagnostics before invasive procedures can help doctors decide how to treat patients and give them a better idea of what might happen, as seen with IDH1 positive gliomas \[[72](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_072)\]. The researchers were able to use a CNN to find chromosomal co-deletion of 1p/19q, MGMT promoter methylation status, and an IDH1 mutation all at the same time \[[73](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_073)\]. Several earlier studies have shown that radiomic systems based on AI can accurately predict the presence of cancer markers and genetic changes \[[74](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_074)\]. With these improvements, neurosurgeons may soon be able to tailor care to each patient based on the mutations they expect \[[75](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_075)\].

Over the past few years, there has been a clear shift from standard ML programs to DL programs. A lot of neural networks are used in deep learning tools. These networks work in a way that is similar to how the brain does. Because of this, these systems have become more reliable and good at spotting complex and nuanced trends. According to the study’s results, the deep learning group had a much lower rate of false-positive results per person when looking for brain tumors compared to the regular ML group. This shows that the deep learning method is getting more accurate over time \[[76](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_076)\].

#### 6.3.1.2 Planning

It is very important for people with brain tumors whose care is being managed to make predictions and describe risks. People who have advanced diseases should not have to go through invasive procedures that do not help them live longer or improve their quality of life. This is bad for their general health. Because of this, it is very important to carefully consider whether surgery is right for some people. It’s hard to guess how long people with cancer in the CNS will live, but this information is very important for the patients and their families. Right now, there are a number of grading systems, such as performance status, that can be used to guess how long someone will survive. However, these systems generally lack the ability to personalize their forecasts \[[77](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_077)\]. ML has demonstrated its ability to effectively forecast the survival outcomes of patients with CNS tumors across several contexts \[[78](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_078), [79](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_079), [80](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_080), [81](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_081), [82](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_082), [83](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_083), [84](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_084)\]. The study conducted by the researchers utilized an artificial neural network to integrate various data elements, including patient age, presence of systemic disease, initial tumor type, and metastasis count, with the aim of predicting the one-year survival rate of patients with brain metastases who received radiosurgery treatment. The model demonstrated superior performance compared to conventional methods in predicting survival. In 2021, researchers conducted a study that showcased the efficacy of a ML platform in precisely forecasting the advancement and reappearance of meningiomas solely based on radiological data \[[85](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_085)\]. It is apparent that AI multivariate programs provide the capability to personalize forecasts, hence facilitating the provision of patient-centered treatment \[[86](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_086)\].

Considerable advancements have also been made in the domain of radiomics as it relates to the process of surgical planning. The surgical management of high-grade glioma, specifically, continues to be a subject of controversy among the academic community. The choice between biopsy and resection procedures lacks substantial support from high quality empirical research. The assessment of cases is typically performed individually, considering the compromise between the benefits of resection and the risk of neurological damage, which occurs in around 10% of resections for GBM \[[87](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_087)\]. Ensuring that the advantages of a particular effort surpass its associated risks is of paramount importance. In this regard, AI has the potential to assist in effectively determining and maintaining this delicate equilibrium. The field has exhibited a lack of consensus, as seen by researcher’s findings that two neurosurgeons were more prone to disagreement rather than agreement over the resectability of a GBM \[[88](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_088)\]. It is very hard to figure out if brain tumors can be surgically removed. There is a chance that high-grade gliomas can spread beyond the edges that can be seen on X-rays. In these situations, finding the limits for resection is therefore very dangerous \[[89](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_089)\]. The goal of the study was to find the five most important anatomical features that can be seen clearly on T1-weighted MRI scans so that doctors could guess how likely it was that the cancer would be removed successfully. The study has led to the creation of a valid grading system that can be used to guess how likely it is that tumor resection will work \[[90](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_090)\]. The successful integration of this system into an AI platform has shown that it can correctly predict the surgical resectability of GBM with a high level of accuracy \[[91](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_091)\]. In the future, AI systems could be an important extra tool for making difficult decisions about surgery options.

Preoperative trajectory planning is an essential component of the surgical operation decision. Brain tumors consistently come into contact with other structures, which are susceptible to intraoperative injury. These regions possess the potential for eloquence, epileptogenicity, or a heightened susceptibility to bleeding. The statement that “the decision holds greater significance than the incision” holds special validity within the realm of neurosurgical procedures for tumor extraction \[[92](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_092)\]. Historically, the discernment of these critical phases of the procedure would be carried out by a human analysis of imaging data, as well as during the course of the operation. The task at hand is characterized by a significant expenditure of time, as evidenced by previous research which has indicated the presence of variability across various experts \[[93](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_093)\]. AI programs have demonstrated efficacy in precisely delineating cancers and local anatomical features \[[94](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_094), [95](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_095)\]. It is worth mentioning that in individuals with CNS tumors, the structure of the brain may undergo substantial distortion, hence posing challenges for conventional segmentation algorithms in terms of interpretation. ML techniques have demonstrated efficacy in the domain of trajectory planning. The researchers conducted a study that focused on the utilization of deep learning algorithms in the context of MRI to accurately identify specific organs at risk in patients who are having radiosurgery. The researchers made a significant finding that their automated technique effectively segregated the brainstem in patients diagnosed with CNS tumors. Furthermore, they saw a noteworthy enhancement in time efficiency when compared to traditional approaches \[[96](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_096)\]. Researchers presented a study where they showcased the application of a CNN that exhibited a significantly higher speed, approximately 30 times faster, compared to existing segmentation platforms. Furthermore, the CNN demonstrated higher levels of accuracy in comparison to the most advanced segmentation platforms currently available \[[97](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_097)\]. Multiple more research have also provided evidence of the efficacy of employing deep learning models for accurate segmentation of cerebral cancers and detection of local structures at risk \[[98](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_098), [99](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_099), [100](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_100), [101](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_101)\]. Moreover, these models have been successfully employed to model tissue deformation during neurosurgical procedures \[[102](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_102)\]. ML systems have been effectively employed for the precise alignment of beams in stereotactic radiosurgery \[[103](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_103)\]. The integration of AI with radiomic programs has the potential to facilitate the creation of a systematic protocol for addressing brain tumors. This capability is exemplified by the ROBOCAST project. Previous investigations have established the viability of planning a trajectory for stereotactic brain biopsies \[[104](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_104)\].

### 6.3.2 Intraoperative phase

The progress of artificial intelligence technology, namely in the area of CV, has resulted in the potential for ML programs to have a beneficial effect on individuals with brain tumors at the intraoperative stage (see [Figure 6.2](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_fig_002)). The main areas of significance are the identification of tumors during surgery and the analysis of workflow.

#### 6.3.2.1 Tumor tissue identification

The accurate identification of cancer in relation to healthy tissue during surgery is a considerable obstacle for neurosurgeons, carrying substantial implications. According to research, the primary factor contributing to cancer recurrence is remaining peripheral tumor tissue that remains undetected and not removed during the surgical procedure \[[105](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_105)\]. Hence, it is not unexpected that in the case of glioma and GBM, a greater extent of tumor excision has been demonstrated to be correlated with increased survival rates \[[106](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_106)\]. The utilization of image guidance has shown beneficial in the identification of tumor site. Nevertheless, it is crucial to acknowledge that the precision of this method can be reduced in the context of surgical interventions due to the displacement of cortical markers \[[107](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_107), [108](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_108)\]. The application of fluorescent tumor markers has been utilized in surgical operations to distinguish between tumor and normal tissue, resulting in varying degrees of effectiveness. Despite this, it can only be used on high-grade tumors and has been shown to lead to partial tumor removal in some cases \[[109](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_109)\]. While tumor was being removed, the researchers looked at how the fluorescent marker 5-aminolevulinic acid (5-ALA) and white light worked side by side. The researchers found that 35% of patients who used 5-ALA still had a tumor after surgery to remove it. That being said, this number went up to 64% when only white light was used \[[110](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_110)\]. It is clear that even with the use of modern fluorescence methods, getting rid of all cancer cells is still hard.

Adding deep learning algorithms to hyperspectral imaging (HSI) is a possible way to quickly find and describe brain tumors before surgery. In HSI, spectroscopy and intraoperative imaging are used together to get a lot of chemical and spatial information about the structures around the tumor \[[111](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_111)\]. A high-resolution camera is placed right above the surgery area as part of this method. Furthermore, the camera is capable of detecting both visible and near-infrared light, enabling the generation of hyperspectral digital images. Small places inside the surgical domain are shown by the pixels in the picture. After that, the digital pictures are put on the platform of deep learning. This platform can tell the difference between tumor tissue and healthy tissue by finding small differences in the “spectral signatures” of the two. The method’s noninvasive features have shown promise in the treatment of different types of cancer \[[112](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_112), [113](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_113), [114](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_114), [115](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_115)\]. To look at hyperspectral pictures (HSIs) for their study, the researchers used a deep learning method with a neural network that has three convolutional layers. Six people who were diagnosed with GBM were treated using this method. The method correctly identified the background 98% of the time, but only 42% of the time did it correctly identify the cancer tissue. The experts examined the technology and found that it had a sensitivity of 88% and a specificity of 100% for determining the difference between healthy and cancerous cells. The method correctly identified the images as not having any cancerous cells \[[116](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_116)\].

New studies show that HSI methods are getting better. Researchers used Raman scattering images during surgery to tell the difference between healthy tissue and cancerous tissue. The researchers came up with a way to find changes in tissue properties like the number of cells, the protein-to-lipid ratio, and the density of axons. The main goal was to identify the cancerous tissue. A sensitivity rate of 97.5% and a precision rate of 98.5% \[[117](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_117)\] show that the tool is very accurate. Previous studies have shown that methods based on AI can help doctors make more accurate diagnoses of brain tumors during surgery \[[118](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_118)\].

#### 6.3.2.2 Workflow

An interesting part of AI is the study of intraoperative process analysis. CV and ML are used by these systems to watch and analyze different parts of surgical procedures, such as stages, tools, phases, anatomy, gestures, and pathologies. A researcher has suggested that using AI to look at processes could have many benefits. These features include improving the surgical plan and path in real time during the intraoperative phase, accurately identifying anatomical structures, sending out timely alerts for high-risk parts of the procedure, making sure that all phases and steps are done the same way, writing detailed operative notes, and helping with simulation and training programs \[[119](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_119), [120](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_120), [121](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_121), [122](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_122)\]. As computers get better, real-time intraoperative guiding may help surgeons. This can show them things like high-risk tracks and places to avoid. There is a good chance that this technology could cut down on problems, surgical mistakes, and operation times \[[123](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_123)\].

Brain tumor surgery is one area where workflow analysis tools have been shown to be useful. After reaching a consensus on the sequential procedures and phases associated with the extraction of pituitary tumors, scholars demonstrated the effectiveness of a CNN in identifying and examining surgical videos of endoscopic transsphenoidal pituitary adenoma removal. According to research \[[124](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_124)\], the platform demonstrated 91% accuracy in detecting certain phases and 75% accuracy in detecting steps. The continuous progress of AI has the potential to significantly influence the domain of surgery in forthcoming years. Specifically, the utilization of intraoperative video analysis and the associated advantages it offers could potentially disrupt established surgical practices to a large extent \[[19](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_019)\]. With the advancement of phase and step recognition platforms, there is a potential for the emergence of AI applications. These applications may encompass real-time decision support systems as well as partially or fully automated phases within diverse procedures. The integration of AI with surgical robotics holds considerable promise in terms of its ability to make a substantial influence on the management of brain tumors. Although the topic of AI robotics in surgical practices is still in its early stages, it presents several potential advantages compared to current methods. These advantages include the ability to withstand tiredness, a decrease in tremors, and an improvement in precision. In the past few decades, there has been an introduction of various neurosurgical robotics \[[125](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_125), [126](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_126), [127](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_127), [128](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_128), [129](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_129), [130](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_130)\]. The Da Vinci surgical robot has garnered significant enthusiasm and potential. The Da Vinci robot is extensively utilized as a surgical robot on a global scale. It operates through a “master-slave” program, enabling the operator to remotely manipulate the robot’s multiple arms for the execution of minimally invasive treatments. A study conducted on cadavers revealed the challenges associated with the execution of minimally invasive cranial microsurgery. These challenges were recognized as including inadequate haptic input, restricted instrument options, and unwieldy arm movements \[[131](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_131)\]. The researchers introduced NeuroArm, a surgical robot that facilitates image-guided microsurgery. The technology exhibits considerable promise as an AI-robotic platform. Currently, there exists a multitude of obstacles, including financial implications, incorporation into existing processes, and the need for supplementary instruction \[[132](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_132)\]. The likelihood of developing and incorporating an autonomous surgical robot with the ability to do human-level surgeries for CNS malignancies is currently low \[[133](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_133)\].

### 6.3.3 Postoperative phase

The ability of artificial intelligence systems to efficiently analyze large volumes of data places them in a favorable position to potentially have a positive impact on the postoperative phase, offering numerous areas where they can be influential (see [Figure 6.2](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_fig_002)). The main areas of influence are inpatient and acute healthcare environments, as well as outpatient and oncological healthcare settings.

#### 6.3.3.1 Inpatient and acute care

The postoperative phase experienced by patients with brain tumors is marked by an increased level of risk and is frequently hindered by a range of problems. The incidence of postoperative complications is impacted by a confluence of both static and dynamic variables, and ML methodologies are especially adept at examining these variables \[[134](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_134)\].

There have been multiple occurrences where the implementation of AI has been demonstrated at the postoperative stage, particularly in areas unrelated to the surgical treatment of brain tumors \[[135](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_135), [136](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_136), [137](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_137)\]. NLP methods were used by researchers to build an ML program that could look at medical data of patients. Subsequently, the software was employed to develop predictive models intended to ascertain the frequency of surgical site infections (SSIs) \[[138](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_138)\]. Researchers employed artificial neural networks to predict potential complications in patients who underwent anterior lumbar fusion, such as venous thromboembolism and SSI. The neural networks achieved a remarkable 95% accuracy rate, much surpassing the 62% accuracy rate of conventional logistic regression approaches. The researchers successfully predicted the incidence of SSIs in individuals undergoing posterior spine fusion surgery. The predictive model had a commendable predictive value of 92.3% \[[139](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_139)\]. The researchers claim that these problems lead to inferior outcomes for patients, worse patient satisfaction, and higher costs \[[140](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_140)\]. The integration of AI in brain tumor surgery has the potential to enhance the prediction and management of various common postoperative complications. The consequences include adverse medication events \[[141](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_141)\], venous thromboembolism \[[142](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_142)\], the development of pressure ulcers \[[143](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_143), [144](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_144)\], incidents of falls \[[145](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_145)\], and episodes of hypoglycemia \[[146](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_146), [147](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_147)\]. These issues are commonly preventable and significantly affect the results of patients. AI has the capacity to reduce the incidence of common postoperative problems.

An increasing level of attention has been noted in the field of ML as it pertains to intensive care units (ICUs). The application of these advancements could potentially aid intensivists in effectively controlling the wide range of physiological data observed in critical care units, as a considerable number of individuals with brain tumors require admittance to the ICU during the duration of recovery. Theoretically, these technologies have the potential to identify the failing patient at an earlier stage compared to conventional approaches \[[148](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_148)\].

#### 6.3.3.2 Outpatient and oncological care

Histological analysis is performed on tumor specimens throughout the postoperative period, acting as a crucial prerequisite for further oncological treatment. Significant advancements have been achieved in the field of histology as a result of the development of artificial intelligence. The standard techniques of histopathological research are based on a series of necessary stages, including specimen preparation, staining procedures, the application of tests, and subsequent examinations \[[149](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_149)\]. The delay in the initiation of rationalized therapies following tissue sample is attributed to the allocation of human resources and the time required for this process. Furthermore, contemporary histological diagnostic methodologies rely on the subjective interpretation of visual patterns by human observers and the examination of cellular morphological features. Notwithstanding efforts towards standardization, this methodology naturally introduces bias as a result of the subjective character of human perception and discrepancies across various histopathologists \[[150](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_150), [151](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_151)\]. The utilization of AI has the capacity to greatly disrupt this particular process, presenting the possibility of accelerated and accurate diagnostics, while also guaranteeing enhanced uniformity and standardization \[[151](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_151)\]. Significant progress has been made in the domain of histological analysis in the discipline of ML over the last decade \[[152](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_152)\]. ML algorithms are capable of analyzing digitized histopathology slides and can identify both macro- and micropatterns. Patterns like area structure, shape, and cellular morphology are part of these patterns. The algorithms are capable of formulating precise histological conclusions by analyzing this data \[[153](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_153)\].

Incorporating AI technologies into the histological diagnosis of brain tumors has the potential to significantly change traditional approaches. First, it’s important to note that AI can use radiomics to predict the grade and molecular expression, which could eliminate the need for a biopsy. This would provide a viable alternative way for diagnosis. After that, AI has the potential to speed up specimen analysis and improve the accuracy of scoring. In addition, using deep learning models can help sort patients into new groups, which can make therapy easier and increase the number of people survival. In conclusion, AI-based algorithms that can predict molecular and cellular markers in brain tumors could make it easier to customize treatments, which could lead to better treatment outcomes and less harm to patients from side effects \[[154](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_154), [155](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_155), [156](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_156), [157](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_157), [158](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_158), [159](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_159), [160](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_160), [161](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_161), [162](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_162)\]. Several studies \[[163](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_163), [164](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_164), [165](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_165)\] have compared the use of AI in histopathology with traditional imaging methods. All of these studies have found that the AI-assisted method is not worse than the traditional methods. In relation to brain tumors, researchers conducted a study whereby they showcased the efficacy of a computer-based analysis system in accurately diagnosing GBM and low-grade glioma through the analysis of digital histopathology slides \[[166](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_166)\]. The utilization of HSI was implemented by researchers in order to detect high-grade glioma on histopathology slides \[[167](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_167)\]. In a separate investigation, scholars utilized a CNN to categorize brain histology samples into two distinct groups: low-grade glioma and high-grade glioma. The CNN achieved an accuracy of 100% for low-grade glioma classification and 98% for high-grade glioma classification. One notable aspect was the implementation of transfer learning. The performance of glioma classification was enhanced with the incorporation of supplementary training dataset slides containing breast cancers into the AI program. According to scientists, this could be highly valuable in the development of ML program for uncommon tumors, where there is a scarcity of training data. Several further researches have demonstrated similarly encouraging findings \[[168](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_168), [169](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_169), [170](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_170), [171](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_171), [172](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_172)\].

The prospective impact of AI in the domain of neurosurgical oncology has the capability to offer sustained benefits to patients during their postoperative recovery phase, surpassing their release from the medical center. In their study, researchers introduced a mobile phone application called Vinehealth that incorporates ML capabilities. This app utilizes data provided by patients to effectively monitor symptoms, offer timely reminders for medication and upcoming appointments, and deliver personalized instructional material \[[173](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_173)\]. ML-based platforms have the potential to acquire a more precise and up-to-date comprehension of patient well-being by facilitating the regular input of data by the patients themselves. On the other hand, when clinic sessions are scheduled with several months in between, there is a potential for the deterioration of patients to remain undetected. There have been advancements in the development of medication management systems that utilize AI technology. These systems are designed with the objective of enhancing drug adherence \[[174](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_174)\]. Similarly, there has been a growing prevalence of biometric monitoring systems in the healthcare sector. These systems employ data, such as continuous monitoring of step count and vital signs, to facilitate a real-time and objective evaluation of the patient’s functional state. Prior research has demonstrated that these systems exhibit the capacity to predict adverse events, hospitalization, and changes in depression scores \[[175](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_175), [176](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_176), [177](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_177), [178](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_178), [179](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_179)\]. In a time marked by rapid technology advancements, it is expected that the incorporation of patients’ mobile phones and Fitbits will emerge as essential elements in the provision of postoperative care. ML algorithms have demonstrated the ability to accurately forecast the likelihood of patient readmission. Several ML algorithms have exhibited significant levels of accuracy in post-spinal surgery scenarios \[[180](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_180), [181](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_181), [182](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_182)\] and various other contexts \[[183](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_183)\]. These algorithms hold potential for future utilization in predicting the likelihood of complications upon discharge for patients with brain tumors.

The optimization of adjuvant therapy during the postoperative period can be accomplished through the utilization of AI programs, with the aim of attaining the highest level of effectiveness. As AI continues to be integrated into brain tumor therapy, there is the possibility for significant customization in the selection of modality, timing, dose, and duration of adjuvant therapy. In a recent study conducted by researchers, a reinforcement ML program was introduced. The program aimed to iteratively optimize the dosage of chemotherapeutic agents in a simulated trial involving patients with GBM \[[184](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_184)\]. Although there is currently no direct evidence available on AI-based chemotherapy regimens for brain tumors, there have been studies conducted on the optimization of chemotherapeutic regimens using AI for other types of primary tumors \[[185](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_185), [186](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_186)\]. AI platform was demonstrated in its ability to optimize the dosage and scheduling of chemotherapy treatments for patients with prostate cancer, utilizing artificial intelligence technology. This was achieved while ensuring compliance with a narrow therapeutic range \[[187](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_187)\]. Furthermore, it has been demonstrated that AI models possess the capability of predicting the response of solid organ cancers to chemotherapy. The potential advantages of improved tumor segmentation in adjuvant radiation have been previously discussed in several studies. The application of immunotherapy in CNS malignancies is now in its preliminary trial phase. Nevertheless, it is expected that AI platforms may have the capacity to contribute to the prediction of immunotherapy response, as well as the optimization of dosage and treatment scheduling in forthcoming times \[[188](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_188)\]. Furthermore, the application of AI has promise in enabling the identification of a wide range of therapeutic approaches. ML methodologies can be utilized for the purpose of conducting high-throughput screening, with the objective of predicting the probability of a tumor cell line manifesting a reaction to newly developed chemotherapeutic drugs \[[189](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_189)\]. The practice of reverse engineering pharmaceuticals has the potential to optimize the traditionally time-consuming drug discovery process, leading to the development of increasingly precise therapeutics for brain cancers \[[190](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_190)\].

The assessment of treatment response plays a crucial role in customizing therapy for individuals. In the field of neuro-oncology, a diverse array of indicators is employed to evaluate the efficacy of treatment. Nevertheless, there is an emerging tendency in both clinical and experimental contexts to employ the Response Assessment in Neuro-oncology (RANO) guidelines for this objective \[[191](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_191)\]. The basis for criterion involves the employment of post-intervention MRI scans in order to assess brain tumors. Following this, the pictures are put through a two-dimensional volumetric study. However, the current methods for evaluating treatment response may not be able to effectively monitor tumors with an anisotropic growth pattern. Therefore, researchers have suggested using artificial neural networks to keep an eye on the volumetric reaction to therapy. The researchers \[[192](#9783111547589_009762.xhtml#b_9783111549729-006_ref_192)\] have shown that such a program could work and have seen better performance and reliability compared to current RANO-based methods for judging response. Future generations of AI may be able to more accurately track how well treatment is working for people with brain tumors.

## 6.4 Conclusion

Computer programs that use AI could completely change how people with brain tumors are treated. This approach will be used at all stages of a patient’s healthcare journey, such as: (1) the initial evaluation before surgery, which includes diagnosing the condition and making a treatment plan; (2) the analysis of tissue samples during the surgery, as well as an evaluation of the workflow during the operation; and (3) the care given right after surgery, as well as the subsequent outpatient and oncological treatment. Using AI could also change the way national guidelines are made and help brain cancer study and treatment progress. In this way, AI might be able to improve patient results in the future. As the creation of AI platforms for brain tumor surgery moves forward, it is very important that reporting standards be followed in clinical trials to build strong proof and reduce bias. The development of intelligent technology is the result of recent technological advances and an extensive amount of research. To make this happen, new computer programs have been designed and human cognitive models for artificial intelligence have been used more effectively. AI technologies are becoming more and more important and are being used in more and more areas of healthcare. These include preoperative medicine, smart operating rooms, robotic interventions, intraoperative management, patient safety maintenance, risk factor identification, and rehabilitation after surgery.
