# Chapter 11 Implementation of AI in pathology

#### Abstract

The increasing use of digital technologies has made it possible to incorporate machine learning and artificial intelligence (AI) into pathology. However, the utilization of these advancements is still in its earliest stages, and no randomized prospective trials have yet been conducted to illustrate the benefits of AI-based diagnostics. The present body of research pertaining to AI in the domain of pathology primarily centers around helping in routine diagnostic tasks and facilitating prognostication. The healthcare industry is just now beginning to acknowledge the potential of AI, a rapidly growing area of modern technology. The field of pathology is expected to have significant effects from the implementation of AI. The growing prevalence of digital pathology (DP) by laboratories is expected to serve as a crucial foundation for the implementation of these technologies, leading to their practical utilization in diagnostic practice. The utilization of AI in the domain of pathology holds promise for the development of image analysis tools. These tools have the ability to function as diagnostic aids or to provide new perspectives on disease biology, complementing the capabilities of human observers. DP has become a well-known concept among pathologists; nonetheless, comprehending the engineering and mathematical principles underlying DP remains challenging for a significant number of pathologists. Computer-aided pathology (CAP) facilitates the diagnostic process for pathologists. However, there are individuals who regard CAP as a potential threat to the pathology profession and have issues about its clinical efficacy. The introduction of DP poses significant challenges for pathologists due to the need to address technical considerations, workflow implications, and information technology infrastructure.

Keywords:  Digital pathology,  computer-aided pathology,  artificial intelligence,  deep learning,  image analysis,  machine learning,  pathology,  

## 11.1 Introduction

The field of health care is intrinsically centered around data, as it includes multiple subdomains that generate data, such as insurance, administration, pharmacy, medical facilities, and diverse clinical specialties \[[1](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_001)\]. Significant quantities of data are created at many tiers of healthcare, offering the opportunity to gain distinctive perspectives on the implementation of medicine on a large scale \[[2](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_002)\]. The implementation of clinical procedures enhanced by artificial intelligence (AI) has significantly advanced the capacity to gather healthcare data \[[3](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_003)\]. Nevertheless, there is a noticeable delay in the implementation of extensive data analytics in these specific areas of the healthcare industry \[[4](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_004), [5](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_005)\]. Automating the analysis of massive datasets is expected to be greatly aided by computer methods that are based on concepts of natural language processing and machine learning (ML). By analyzing data, these algorithms may be able to identify trends that improve the comprehension of medical procedures and lead to more efficient treatment delivery \[[6](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_006)\].

Despite the abundance of data-generating sources in the domain of healthcare, researchers in the field of AI tend to concentrate their efforts on the data produced within the framework of regular clinical practice. Clinicians produce substantial quantities of unorganized data, such as medical data recorded during interactions with the patient. Nevertheless, particularly in industrialized countries, healthcare professionals largely depend on radiography and pathology as crucial tools for informing the diagnosis, treatment, prognosis, and overall care of patients \[[7](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_007), [8](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_008)\]. Competence in the use of computed tomography and magnetic resonance imaging, two essential technological tools in the area of radiology, is demonstrated by radiologists \[[9](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_009), [10](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_010)\]. The practice of pathology is being significantly influenced by the growing prominence of technological innovation. Both radiology and pathology are medical disciplines that heavily rely on images to provide patient care through the analysis and interpretation of specialized image data. While radiology has made significant progress in the digitalization and administration of medical images, pathology is also making strides in this direction \[[11](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_011), [12](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_012)\]. The significant dependence on visual representations and digitalization renders these two domains within the healthcare field particularly appealing to researchers in the field of AI, as they provide fertile ground for the exploration and evaluation of novel concepts in the realm of imaging AI research. Over the course of the past decade, there has been a significant amount of research and advancements focused on imaging AI systems. The convergence of ample imaging data, continuously expanding cost-effective and robust computational capabilities, and progressing algorithmic AI research render radiology and pathology as highly promising domains for transformative innovation in healthcare AI utilization in the future \[[13](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_013)\-[15](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_015)\].

Pathology practice includes both anatomic pathology, which relies heavily on images, and clinical laboratory medicine. There have been substantial improvements in the processing time for patient test results thanks to the widespread use of automation in clinical laboratory medicine during the last several decades. The utilization of genomes and other -omics data at a molecular level has garnered significant attention in developing domains like precision medicine, particularly in the context of diagnosis and prognosis \[[16](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_016)\]. The emerging field of omics technologies has a prominent position in the field of big data, characterized by the generation of extensive volumes of patient data during each interaction \[[17](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_017), [18](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_018)\]. The discipline of bioinformatics centers around the utilization of algorithmic computing techniques to effectively handle and analyze omics data within diverse therapeutic contexts. Applying AI-based algorithms to omics data in the context of patient health care is becoming more and more of an interest for AI researchers \[[19](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_019)\]. One major challenge with AI-enabled healthcare is the integration of patient imaging and genetic data. One of the primary goals is to use these data to generate novel insights into clinical outcomes and improve the care of patients \[[20](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_020)\]. There are currently several such efforts in this way.

The utilization of AI-based algorithms in medical care holds substantial promise for efficiently handling and analyzing large volumes of data. However, it is crucial to acknowledge the notable drawbacks associated with deploying such potent technology in the absence of essential ethical and moral protections \[[21](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_021)\-[24](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_024)\]. There is a growing sense of concern about the unregulated utilization of AI in the field of medicine, especially in relation to ethical considerations encompassing patient confidentiality, the amplification of racial and gender disparities, and the potential impact on patient safety results. The primary focus of AI ethics pertains to the use of AI tools to facilitate ethical and moral progress for the collective welfare of society. The misuse of AI technology encompasses both overarching problems that cut across all disciplines and more specialized ones that are specific to fields. Establishing ethical guidelines for the right deployment of AI technologies requires the collaboration of domain specialists, such as practicing pathologists. The promotion of ethical AI research, development, and implementation involves various essential participants, including a diverse group of individuals and institutions. They include scientists studying AI, pathologists, doctors, institution management, professional organizations, and significantly, the patients themselves. [Figure 11.1](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_fig_001) depicts the AI ethical paradigm and the participants in this ever-evolving development process.

![Figure 11.1: The study of ethical considerations within the domain of AI applied to pathology has identified key contributors and emergent challenges of significance. Comprehending the ethical concerns depicted in the central image is crucial for establishing a comprehensive framework for the incorporation of AI in the field of pathology. These issues exhibit a degree of ubiquity across diverse academic disciplines, while yet possessing distinct characteristics that are exclusive to the context of each respective profession. Relationships among many different groups, such as patients, AI researchers, pathologists, and institutions, are crucial to the development of a comprehensive framework for understanding AI ethics in pathology.](graphic/converted/b_9783111549729-011_fig_001.png)

Figure 11.1: The study of ethical considerations within the domain of AI applied to pathology has identified key contributors and emergent challenges of significance. Comprehending the ethical concerns depicted in the central image is crucial for establishing a comprehensive framework for the incorporation of AI in the field of pathology. These issues exhibit a degree of ubiquity across diverse academic disciplines, while yet possessing distinct characteristics that are exclusive to the context of each respective profession. Relationships among many different groups, such as patients, AI researchers, pathologists, and institutions, are crucial to the development of a comprehensive framework for understanding AI ethics in pathology.

AI has become a significant field in the domain of technology in recent years. AI is now present in nearly every facet of modern life. Its presence can be observed in several domains, ranging from personalized movie recommendations on streaming platforms like Netflix, to the utilization of speech recognition technology for efficient dictation purposes. Moreover, AI is also making notable advancements within the medical sector, with its new applications holding significant potential for transformative impact. There is a substantial global investment being directed toward the research and development of AI. Although the general public is increasingly aware of AI, it is crucial to acknowledge that AI is not a recent development. Efforts to systematically represent cognitive processes in a rational fashion have persisted over the course of several decades. In 1950, Turing proposed the notion that genuine AI will possess the capability to mimic human cognition to such an extent that it would be impossible to discern between the two \[[25](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_025)\]. Recent research has moved its attention to the topic of whether AI can mimic human cognition and conduct. The utilization of AI in the realm of medicine for the purpose of assisting in diagnostic processes was initially observed during the 1970s, primarily serving as a means of instructional support. One notable example is the ‘MYCIN’ expert system, which was developed to detect the presence of bacteria responsible for infections. The system operated on a methodology comparable to contemporary mobile health applications, employing a series of binary inquiries to deduce a potential diagnosis \[[26](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_026)\].

ML and deep learning (DL), in particular, have come a long way in the last 20 years. The current focus on AI applications in healthcare is greatly aided by advancements in computer capability. Collaboration with organizations is frequently crucial for the advancement of tools, as exemplified by the partnership between Moorfields Eye Hospital in London and a commercial entity. This collaboration resulted in the development of an AI system capable of accurately interpreting retinal optical coherence tomography scans for various retinal diseases, matching, or even surpassing the accuracy of ophthalmologists \[[27](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_027)\]. Imperial College London collaborated with Cancer Research UK to establish a comparable relationship, resulting in the generation of an AI system that has exhibited superior performance in identifying breast cancer on mammography as compared to radiologists \[[28](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_028)\].

The field of histopathology has been perceived as lagging in this domain as it has primarily prioritized the transition toward a completely digital workflow. Nevertheless, it is noteworthy that this specialty is rapidly making progress in narrowing the gap. The UK Government Industrial Strategy Challenge Fund has allocated a substantial amount of financing toward the development of AI in the area of pathology, hence establishing AI centers of excellence. Multiple consortia are working on digital pathology (DP) and AI development. In the southeast of England, the Midlands, and Northern Ireland, there is a cooperation called PathLAKE that operates data lakes for analytics, knowledge, and education related to pathology. The iCAIRD consortium is based in Scotland; it is an industrial center for AI research in digital diagnostics. Furthermore, the Northern Pathology Imaging Cooperative (NPIC) is situated in the northern region of England. When it comes to diagnostic purposes in pathology, the use of DL algorithms has regulatory approval. The initial tools to receive such clearance are utilized to identify prostate cancer and have been awarded the CE marking for in vitro diagnostics (CE IVD) \[[29](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_029)\].

## 11.2 AI and digital image analysis

AI includes methods that teach machines to solve problems efficiently without human intervention. The domain of AI research and development falls within the purview of computer science. In contrast, ML is focused on programming AI through the use of specific methods \[[30](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_030)\]. In certain cases, a differentiation is occasionally drawn between conventional ML and more recent methodologies, such as DL. Several advancements over the past decade have contributed to the substantially increased use of AI. These include enhancements in algorithms, improvements in hardware capabilities, and a substantial increase in the availability of data. These advancements occurred simultaneously with the shift in pathology toward greater digitization.

Significant advancements have been made in the area of computer vision, also known as machine vision, particularly in the area of automated computer-based processing of visual input. The use of artificial neural networks (ANNs) is crucial to the success of these solutions. These intricate algorithms are founded on the fundamental principle of mathematically simulating biological neural networks. These models are usually trained with massive amounts of photos from popular datasets like the highly esteemed ImageNet dataset \[[31](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_031)\].

The transfer learning phase involves using the pretrained networks with appropriate adjustments to address different types of concerns.

There has been an increasing evaluation of AI-assisted methodologies within the medical field, particularly in the realm of imaging disciplines. The trend suggests a potential for broader adoption and utilization of such approaches in the foreseeable future. In the field of radiology, some companies have developed solutions that aim to meet certain regular diagnostic inquiries. However, it is worth noting that none of these products have achieved widespread adoption on a large scale \[[32](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_032)\]. In the field of pathology, the process occurs with even greater caution, as the utilization of full slide scanners for routine diagnosis remains rare. The potential factors contributing to such a phenomenon are multifaceted, encompassing a wide range of issues such as the exorbitant expenses associated with investing, concerns pertaining to security, and misgivings expressed by pathologists. However, specific business and academic institutions are actively involved in pioneering initiatives within the field. The utilization of digital interpretation has been mostly observed in pathology institutes situated in Leeds, Pittsburgh, Utrecht, and New York. Additionally, certain companies have already started delivering pathology solutions that are particular to certain market groups and have obtained CE certification \[[33](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_033)\]. In addition to the process of scanning slides in every aspect, digital reporting encompasses various elements. These include the utilization of barcodes to automatically collect case numbers, the aid of recognition of voice recognition for dictating findings, and the computerized transmission of the results to the hospital information system (HIS), in addition to other functions. In a blinded randomized noninferiority trial with a sample size of 1,992 cases, digital interpretation by computer was shown to produce results that were similar to analog diagnosis by microscopy \[[34](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_034)\]. Researchers also used computational models to estimate that a university center may save US$12.4 million over 5 years in operating costs. Out of the whole amount, a sum of US$5.4 million can be attributed to the enhanced precision of diagnoses, which subsequently led to savings in treatment expenses \[[35](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_035)\]. Furthermore, researchers observed reduced processing durations, particularly in instances involving complicated scenarios \[[36](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_036)\]. At the moment, there is a dearth of comparable studies for the application of AI in the field of pathology, although this limitation is not unique to digital-centric methodologies. The application of AI in facilitating routine diagnosis within the field of classical pathology, along with the prospect of implementing novel computer-assisted diagnostic methods, demands consideration (see [Figure 11.2](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_fig_002)).

![Figure 11.2: An illustrative DL model that has been specifically developed to discern between colorectal cancer and normal colorectal mucosa. The image on the left illustrates the standard histology input, whereas the image on the right displays the tissue emphasized according to the categorization result produced by the AI algorithm. Initially, the ANN classifies distinct image parts, also known as tiles. Subsequently, each tile is assigned a color code according to its respective prediction probability. There is an increased likelihood of the class “tumour” being represented by the color red, while the class “normal mucosa” is more likely to be represented by the color green \[36\].](graphic/converted/b_9783111549729-011_fig_002.png)

Figure 11.2: An illustrative DL model that has been specifically developed to discern between colorectal cancer and normal colorectal mucosa. The image on the left illustrates the standard histology input, whereas the image on the right displays the tissue emphasized according to the categorization result produced by the AI algorithm. Initially, the ANN classifies distinct image parts, also known as tiles. Subsequently, each tile is assigned a color code according to its respective prediction probability. There is an increased likelihood of the class “tumour” being represented by the color red, while the class “normal mucosa” is more likely to be represented by the color green \[[36](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_036)\].

## 11.3 AI to support routine pathological diagnosis

AI and ML algorithms have proven to be very valuable in tackling repetitive inquiries that possess a restricted level of intricacy. Routine pathological diagnosis encompasses various aspects, including the examination of tissue specimens obtained from extensive screening programs like colorectal cancer screening. Additionally, it involves the evaluation of cases where numerous comparable slides are provided, such as sections of prostate tissues. If pathologists were relieved from time-consuming and repetitive screening activities using AI algorithms, they would have the opportunity to direct their attention toward more advanced responsibilities, such as the evaluation of predictive or prognostic biomarkers within specific clinical observations. The historical roots of research pertaining to the manual extraction of picture properties and classical morphometry can be identified as originating in the 1980s \[[37](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_037), [38](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_038)\]. Simultaneously, efforts were made to progress the statistical approaches, and the initial utilization of these enhanced techniques was observed in the field of pathology. In a retrospective examination, the ability to distinguish between breast tumor and normal parenchyma of the breast was demonstrated with a high accuracy of 98.8% across multiple benchmark datasets. It was achieved through the utilization of feature extraction techniques and a classifier based on support vector machine models \[[39](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_039)\]. Prior studies have been conducted to examine the occurrence of prostate tumors and oral carcinoma of squamous cells \[[40](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_040), [41](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_041)\]. Accuracy ratings of 70.8% and 99.1% were attained, respectively. The use of ANNs and DL techniques shows potential for enhancing classification accuracy. In a separate retrospective analysis, researchers investigated specifically targeting the identification of metastases in lymph node in individuals diagnosed with breast tumor. The researchers compared various models that were entered in an annual programming contest focused on biomedical imaging. An impressive area under the curve (AUC) value of 0.994 on the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) demonstrated the amazing efficacy of the convolutional neural network (CNN) technique, which was created from Google technology. The average false positive rate shown by the results was 1.25. The ROC curve is commonly used to depict the relationship between sensitivity and 1 – specificity. The AUROC is commonly used as a measure of the predictive capability of a model. In addition, the researchers compared the models with the best performance to the pathologists’ abilities. The investigation’s findings indicated that experienced pathologists achieved slightly higher accuracy values (AUC 0.966) than the algorithms (AUC 0.960) when not bound by time constraints. However, when the interpreting pathologist faced time constraints, a notable decrease in these values was noted (AUC 0.810) \[[42](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_042)\]. The assessment of diagnostic accuracy was carried out with and without the application of AI in an independent study related to the same inquiry. The sensitivity of identifying micrometastases in challenging situations was found to increase from 83.3% when relying solely on human assessment to 91.2% when combining human assessment with computer assistance (*p* = 0.023). Furthermore, the researchers observed that the combined efforts of humans and machines resulted in greater overall accuracy when compared to the accuracy reached by either humans or machines alone \[[43](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_043)\].

## 11.4 Applications of AI in pathology: the current perspective

### 11.4.1 Improve workflow efficacy

Numerous applications of AI and DP remain untapped, however they collectively provide shared prospective advantages. The use of DP has many benefits, the most important of which are the standardization and improvement of diagnostic accuracy. Additionally, it serves to optimize workflow efficiency, thereby addressing the mounting pressure faced by pathologists due to rising demands. Numerous AI algorithms are designed to operate on sections that have been stained with hematoxylin and eosin (H&E), eliminating the need for supplementary staining or testing procedures. In the 1990s, there were endeavors to utilize ML techniques to assist in the human interpretation of imaging, which demonstrated potential \[[44](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_044)\]. Nevertheless, the performance of computer-aided diagnosis systems during the 2000s fell short of anticipated outcomes \[[45](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_045)\]. The effectiveness of image analysis has been greatly enhanced with the advent of DL, the enhancement of computational capabilities and parallel processing, and the development of more advanced ANNs. As a result, AI pathology research has increasingly focused on studying picture analysis approaches \[[46](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_046)\]. With the development of a streamlined DP workflow, the application of AI and image analysis to the area of pathology holds great promise. It is widely known that DP has several benefits, including improved service reliability, increased efficiency, and higher quality results. Although the utilization of such technology is on the rise, it has not yet achieved widespread adoption \[[47](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_047)\].

The utilization of AI image analysis can serve as a method for prioritizing cases, distinguishing cases with a high probability of being benign, or drawing attention to cases with a high likelihood of being malignant for the pathologist’s consideration. This facilitates a more efficient and optimized allocation of tasks within a department that experiences high levels of activity. One new application of CNNs is the classification of malignancies like lung cancer. A research investigation was undertaken to assess the prospective therapeutic application of a DL system for processing whole slide images (WSIs), utilizing carcinoma of basal cells, prostate cancer, and breast cancer tumors in lymph nodes as the training dataset. According to the study \[[48](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_048)\], pathologists utilizing this technology would have the capability to eliminate between 65 to 75% of slides, while still maintaining a sensitivity rate of 100% in the diagnosis and reporting of cases. The implementation of such strategy has the potential to enhance safety measures. For instance, it may involve comprehensive screening of all cases, potentially giving priority to identifying malignant instances. Additionally, it could involve emphasizing critical regions and conducting thorough reviews to ensure that no diagnoses have been overlooked. As previously indicated, the initial regulatory approval for a DL AI tool in the domain of pathology was obtained in Europe. This tool, which has been certified for clinical use under the CE-IVD certification, specifically focuses on the diagnosis of prostate cancer.

The need for second opinions from additional pathologists may be obviated if well vetted AI systems were to effectively fulfill such a role. These techniques might potentially confirm diagnoses that are already deemed confident, thus rendering routine double reporting ineffective or less integral to reporting requirements. The utilization of the pathologist’s computer as a secondary opinion has the potential to eliminate the necessity for pathologist double reporting. The potential of utilizing duplicate reporting of mammograms in the context of UK breast cancer screening has been demonstrated, revealing a significant reduction of 88% in the workload for the second reporter \[[49](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_049)\]. Although there is a possibility that AI could manage basic second views, it is improbable that AI will possess the capability to provide complicated or expert second pathology opinions for difficult or uncommon situations that necessitate substantial skill, analysis, and clinicopathological connection in the near future.

### 11.4.2 Consistency of assessments

The discipline of diagnostic pathology is significantly dependent on the subjective interpretation as well as pattern recognition skills of human specialists, leading to inherent difficulties associated with subjectivity and diversity across various observers. Diagnostic diversity amongst pathologists can be observed in the context of prostate cancer, as evidenced by previous studies \[[50](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_050), [51](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_051)\]. The reliability of Gleason grading, an important prognostic indicator in prostate cancer, is sometimes compromised due to the inherent challenge of delineating discrete grades within a continuous spectrum of glandular production. This issue arises, in part, from the arbitrary demarcation of borders in the grading system. The process of Gleason grading, normally carried out by highly trained urological pathologists, has been automated through the use of DL algorithms. When compared to human pathologists, these technologies have been shown to be much more effective. Researchers found that a DL system has an accuracy rate of 0.70 (*p* = 0.002), compared to a general pathologist’s 0.61 (*p* = 0.002). This measurement was based on the level of precise agreement with specialist pathologists \[[52](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_052)\]. The DL system has shown notable improvements in sensitivity and specificity when distinguishing between Grade Groups, as well as in accurately quantifying factors such as the percentage of grade 4 disease. DL algorithms demonstrated the capability to discern the relative positioning of a case inside a specific grade, whether it leaned toward a lower or higher grade. As an example, DL has the potential to provide valuable insights in distinguishing between different subcategories under the Gleason grade 3 classification. This could involve determining if a particular case aligns more closely with the typical grade 3 (3.3) or exhibits characteristics that are indicative of a higher grade, such as grade 4.22. This type of insight surpasses the capabilities of human observers and has the potential to assist in elucidating the reasons behind the varying clinical outcomes observed among patients within a specific grade. An additional study on AI and prostate cancer found that it was quite accurate (AUC = 0.997) in differentiating between benign and malignant biopsies. In addition, the AI system’s Gleason grading ability was comparable to that of a panel of professional pathologists \[[53](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_053)\]. The incorporation of a uniform categorization framework inside an AI-based image analysis system can offer numerous benefits, notably in terms of developing consistent diagnoses and grading practices. Additionally, it could improve the outcome prediction models’ dependability.

### 11.4.3 IHC analysis

Multiple image analysis technologies are already accessible for the measurement of immunohistochemistry (IHC)-derived biomarkers. Within specific healthcare institutions, there exist algorithms that have obtained clearance from the FDA and CE for IVD use. These algorithms were developed to evaluate a limited set of pathological characteristics, such as the existence of ER, HER-2, PR, and Ki-67 in breast cancer cases. Furthermore, there exists the possibility of standardizing companion diagnostic testing, such as PDL1 \[[54](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_054)\]. The potential of AI is based on its capacity to enhance the analysis of diagnostic IHC testing. Furthermore, the potential of AI in the realm of clinical trials is evident, as it may be employed to standardize baseline entry tests or endpoints through the utilization of image analysis, hence reducing reliance on subjective human assessment \[[55](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_055)\].

### 11.4.4 Improving accuracy in cell counting

According to the statement, AI demonstrates the ability to detect significant characteristics in slides with a level of accuracy similar to that of pathologists. This capability offers potential enhancements in both the efficiency and reliability of diagnostic procedures. With the advancement of image analysis and the incorporation of larger training datasets, there is a potential for these technologies to assume the responsibility of monotonous activities, such as counting, from pathologists, thereby enhancing reproducibility. Consequently, this would allow pathologists to allocate their attention toward more intricate responsibilities.

The task of determining an exact count of cell types from a slide poses a difficulty for pathologists, as it is commonly presented as an estimated ratio relative to the entire tissue. Precise quantification of cellular populations can be of significant value in illness scenarios where the immune response serves as a prognostic indicator, or when assessing tumor load for genetic analysis. This use of AI has the ability to be more objective than a pathologist doing a cell count, which could lead to the discovery of new prognostic variables \[[56](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_056)\]. AI has the capability to introduce uncertainty regarding the applicability of previously established prognostic variables in certain situations. For example, one study used a CNN that was shown to detect mitoses with a degree of accuracy similar to that of human experts, with almost no variation \[[57](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_057)\]. The study further revealed that the application of CNN to breast cancer cases demonstrated minimal prognostic significance in terms of mitotic count for triple negative breast tumors \[[58](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_058)\]. Cancers including colorectal, breast, and melanoma have recognized tumor infiltrating lymphocytes (TILs) as a predictive marker. For instance, the RCPath reporting dataset for melanoma has incorporated TIL count as a fundamental data element. There is substantial variation among various observers regarding the categorization of TILs into three categories: absent, brisk, and non-brisk \[[59](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_059), [60](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_060)\]. AI holds the potential to offer enhanced precision in evaluating these criteria. Nevertheless, it is imperative to develop novel criteria, protocols, validation methodologies, and maintain ongoing quality assurance measures to facilitate the shift from human review to AI assessment \[[61](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_061), [62](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_062)\].

## 11.5 Other applications of AI in pathology

### 11.5.1 AI-assisted image analysis for prognosis prediction

There are extensive diagnostic inquiries in pathology that use patient prognoses and treatment response predictions to inform clinical practice. To identify biomarkers that can predict or indicate a patient’s prognosis, additional IHC or molecular biological investigations, including next generation sequencing, are often used. The possibility for a substantial revolution exists through the incorporation of AI with DP. Algorithms using AI can identify subvisual structural features that are beyond the human eye’s quantitative capacity. As a result, this ability allows for the creation of a new classification of biomarkers that are based on morphology and possess prognostic or predictive significance. A retrospective study showed that using feature extraction and ML technologies on H&E-stained slides was an efficient way to evaluate the prognosis of breast cancer patients \[[63](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_063)\]. The extraction of image attributes was performed in an automated manner, resulting in the identification of several properties at both the cellular and tissue levels. This link between the model’s predictive capacities and patient survival was shown by the log-rank test to be statistically significant (*p* < 0.001), regardless of other clinical or pathological risk factors. Patients deemed high-risk were appropriately recognized by the algorithm, and their 5 year survival rate was 68.7%. On the other hand, it is worth noting that the five-year survival percentage for patients classified as low-risk was found to be 84.5%. Furthermore, using statistical analysis, the histological criteria that had the greatest impact on the respective classification were determined. A comparable approach, rooted in DL, was elucidated for colorectal tumor \[[64](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_064)\] as well as other medical disorders. Initially, the goal of image processing was accomplished using a deep convolutional network. A recurrent neural network that incorporates long short-term memory capabilities was trained using the output of the first network. Previous retrospective proof-of-concept studies demonstrated the method’s capacity to detect the molecular subtype of bladder cancer and microsatellite instability in gastrointestinal tumors \[[65](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_065), [66](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_066)\].

### 11.5.2 AI-based analysis of genetic data

A high likelihood of observing concurrent changes in the physical characteristics of cancer tissue, specifically those caused by a mutation in a critical gene, is implied by the concept of genotype-phenotype coupling. In the future, molecular pathology data processing will heavily rely on AI technologies, which can already predict molecular or clinical features from histomorphological data. An illustrative example may be discerned in the classification of DNA methylation patterns in lung squamous cell carcinomas. In this process, DL algorithms are used to differentiate between distinct cases of lung cancer and metastases from head and neck carcinoma \[[67](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_067)\]. Likewise, AI methodologies are gaining growing importance within the domain of multiomics-based molecular cancer classifications \[[68](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_068)\]. Despite the absence of current therapeutic applications, there is growing evidence indicating the prospective complementary importance of intricate molecular and morphological profiles \[[69](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_069)\].

### 11.5.3 Integration of histomorphological, molecular pathological, and oncological data

The AI methodologies enable the prediction of certain molecular or clinical characteristics using histomorphological images, as well as the establishment of pathological classifications using molecular data. One potentially effective strategy for enhancing the accuracy of tumor disease prognosis or prediction involves leveraging a diverse range of data modalities as inputs to an AI model. In such a setting, it is reasonable to contemplate a specific paradigm pertaining to pathology, whereby the integration of immunohistochemical, histological, and molecular data might be undertaken. Researchers \[[70](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_070)\] conducted a retrospective investigation on patients diagnosed with glioma, providing a fascinating picture. In their study, the researchers used genetic data alongside imaging data to enhance their findings. When comparing the accuracy of prognostic predictions made using image data with those made using mutation data alone, the combined results showed a significant improvement (*p* = 0.0106). This integration led to a 5% increase in the C index, which improved from 0.754 to 0.801. The integration of many biomarkers, encompassing morphological, laboratory, radiographic, and clinical indicators, alongside genetic and proteomic data, presents a substantial obstacle within the domain of personalized medicine. The complexity of this information necessitates the utilization of AI methods to effectively address this challenge. Nevertheless, the implementation of such a system for normal clinical applications is still distant due to the significant requirement for IT and clinical skills, as well as the utmost importance of data quality.

## 11.6 Incorporation of AI-based digital pathology

At present, DP poses a formidable threat to the pathology scientific community. DP is a significant technological advancement that has transformed the traditional methodology of microscope-based pathology, a discipline with a longstanding history spanning over a century. Since the introduction of DP, the field has undergone a major shift in the visualization of pathological pictures used for diagnostic purposes, shifting the traditional microscope-based approach to a computer screen-based approach. Moreover, the emergence of DP has resulted in a transformation in storage media, substituting conventional glass slides with digitized picture data. The utilization of computer servers or cloud systems for transmitting digitalized pathologic images facilitates a reconfiguration of the temporal and spatial aspects of pathologic diagnosis, as it permits their widespread distribution via the Internet. Furthermore, the utilization of ML techniques facilitates the development and implementation of software systems that may actively and efficiently aid in the process of diagnosis.

## 11.7 Digital pathology and computer-aided pathology

DP was originally defined as the procedure of converting WSIs into a digital format utilizing modern slide scanning technology. However, the term “digital pathology” has evolved to encompass many AI-based methods for tasks such as identification, diagnosis, segmentation, and recognition of digitalized pictures \[[32](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_032)\]. A whole histopathologic glass slide can be digitally captured and displayed with microscopic detail using the process known as WSI \[[11](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_011)\]. Significant developments in WSI technology have been made in the last twenty years. These include faster scan times, more capacity for scanners, smaller image files, and wider commercial availability. Hospital systems like picture archiving communication systems (PACS), electronic medical records (EMR), and laboratory information systems (LIS) (also called the pathology order communication system) have become more stable due to the development of effective image management systems and the seamless integration of interfaces among them. There has also been progress in streaming technology and the successful deployment of cost-effective storage systems for handling large amounts of visual data \[[71](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_071)\]. Using specimens obtained from 1,992 patients with various cancer types, the study compared the diagnostic accuracy of digitalized images to that of microscopic images. The evaluation team consisted of 16 surgical pathologists. The results showed that digitalized WSIs were just as accurate as light microscopy-based methods in terms of diagnosis. Supporting this, when compared to the reference standard, there was a statistically significant discordance rate of 4.9% for WSI and 4.6% for microscopy \[[34](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_034)\].

A variety of approaches that employ computational diagnostic systems or software for the analysis of pathologic images comprise computer-aided pathology (CAP), also known as computer-assisted pathology, computational pathology, or computer-aided pathologic diagnostics \[[9](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_009)\]. According to the Digital Pathology Association, computational pathology encompasses more than only computer-based techniques for image processing. In contrast, it includes the use of AI methods in pathology to analyze patient samples by integrating pathologic images and metadata from many sources. The effectiveness of computer-aided diagnostic mechanics has witnessed significant enhancements due to the advancements in AI and computer vision technologies. The utilization of digitized WSIs has proven to be advantageous in enabling the advancement of computer-aided diagnostic tools. This was achieved through the implementation of AI techniques that emulate intelligent behavior in machines \[[72](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_072)\].

Efforts are currently underway to develop effective CAP diagnosis tools through the utilization of AI techniques, with a specific focus on DL models \[[73](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_073)\].

## 11.8 Application of digital pathology

DP involves the utilization of digital scanners to generate digitalized pathologic images, which are then used for various pathologic activities. These activities cover a wide range of topics related to the field, including making primary diagnoses on computer screens, consulting through telepathology, using imaging analysis software for morphometry, taking part in conferences with other disciplines and teaching students, performing quality assurance operations, and using the College of American Pathologists’ improved diagnostic guidelines.

Recently, preliminary diagnoses using digitized pathologic images performed on computer monitor screens have been given practical approval by the FDA of the EU, the USA, and Japan \[[74](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_074), [75](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_075)\]. There have been a plethora of validation studies comparing DP’s diagnostic accuracy to that of traditional microscopic diagnosis within the last decade \[[76](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_076)\]. WSI has not been found to be less accurate in diagnostics than traditional microscopy, according to most research. However, most studies had small sample numbers and a level of evidence that was fairly low, ranging from III to IV. As a result, a proper internal validation study is required to assess the diagnostic agreement between conventional microscopic diagnosis and WSI. Such research, in accordance with the standards proposed by prominent research organizations and top countries, must be undertaken before DP can be applied in specific labs.

Telepathology is a technology framework that enables the diagnosis of pathology through the transmission of real-time pathology images over web connections. The objective is accomplished through the utilization of a tiny system that incorporates a remote-controlled, motorized stage. The term “telediagnosis” has a specific scope of application, primarily in clinical scenarios when a pathological diagnosis is conducted at a distant hospital lacking pathologists. The advent of recent advancements in WSI technology has facilitated the expedited and precise gathering and dissemination of high-quality digital images. The field of telepathology has undergone significant advancements, enabling the seamless use, dissemination, and interchange of DP data across diverse platforms and devices through cloud-based systems. Due to the widespread availability of technology, ensuring network security and protecting personal information have become increasingly crucial \[[77](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_077)\].

The introduction of digital processing is expected to enhance the speed and efficiency of morphometric analysis and computer-aided product design (CAPD) approaches. The Ki-67 labeling index is well recognized as a significant prognostic marker in breast cancer. To provide precise and consistent morphometric analysis, several ML-based image analysis software programs have been created. Complex pathological tasks, such as detecting microtumor metastases in sentinel lymph nodes, identifying mitosis in breast cancer cases, and using the Gleason score for prostate biopsy, are best handled by DL. Also, the abundance of training data made available by Digital Pathology (DP) greatly improves the application of DL to the interpretation of diseased pictures.

Digital Pathology (DP) presents a novel prospect for transformative advancements in the realm of education and the facilitation of multidisciplinary conferences. Thus, the technology facilitates the seamless dissemination of pathological data, hence streamlining the process of creating educational and conference materials. The primary purpose of incorporating laboratory automation technologies and implementing tracking identifying codes in the domain of data processing is to effectively reduce the frequency of human errors and improve patient safety. DP also facilitates the process of reviewing stored slides for pathological examination. The implementation of CAPD systems that use DL techniques enables the efficient and streamlined execution of diagnostic assessments, hence facilitating the expeditious completion of quality assurance tasks with reduced human effort. DL possesses the capability to be utilized for the evaluation of diagnostic mistakes and the assessment of staining quality in histology slides.

When integrated with pertinent medical information, including EMRs, public health data and resources, HIS, imaging technologies like PACS, and genetic data such as next-generation sequencing, Digital Pathology (DP) is instrumental in driving substantial progress in medical technology.

## 11.9 Using a digital pathology system in a clinical setting

The rapid progress of WSI technology has enabled the utilization of digital pathology systems (DPSs) in the field of pathology. The utilization of WSI in clinical environments encompasses several applications, such as primary diagnosis, intraoperative frozen section analysis, expert consultation, clinicopathologic conferences, teaching, remote diagnosis, and quality assurance. The digital pathology system, which has been specifically developed for IVD applications, has a wide range of essential elements. These elements include digital slide scanners, systems for the maintenance and viewing of archives, and a smooth interface with both hospital information systems (HIS) and LIS. Image viewing software frequently integrates image analysis tools. Pathologists analyze WSIs and make diagnoses by utilizing a DPS equipped with appropriate software, hardware, and hospital networks.

Recent WSI scanners facilitate the fast digitization of complete glass slides, producing high-resolution WSI. Nonetheless, variations persist in scanning length, image resolution, scan error rate, and image quality among different WSI scanners. WSI scanners exhibit variations in terms of their functionality and capabilities, and they are common for scanner providers to offer accompanying image readers \[[78](#9783111547589_018653.xhtml#b_9783111549729-011_ref_078)\]. When selecting a WSI scanner for clinical diagnosis, it is essential to consider the following factors: The elements to examine include: (1) the quantification of slide volume, (2) the classification of specimen types (such as tissue section slides, cytology slides, or hematopathology smears), (3) the feasibility of z-stack scanning (also referred to as focus stacking), (4) the specifications for oil-immersion scanning in the laboratory, (5) the specifications for both fluorescence scanning and bright field in the laboratory, (6) the characteristics of the glass slides utilized (e.g., wet slides or slides of atypical dimensions), (7) the readability of slide barcodes, (8) the existence of spatial constraints within the laboratory, (9) viewing and managing images with the vendor-supplied viewer and management system, (10) integration in both directions with preexisting computer networks, (11) method of transmission (e.g., HL7 and XML) between LIS and DPS, (12) regardless of whether the picture-viewing software is kept on the server or on each client machine’s hard drive, (13) whether or if the viewer is accessible via mobile devices, and (14) open or closed system.

## 11.10 Conclusion

The field of AI has undergone substantial expansion and acknowledgment in the last 10 years, resulting in its extensive utilization across many sectors. The domain of pathology possesses considerable promise for the incorporation of AI within the healthcare industry, especially as innovations in digital diagnostic methodologies progress. The principal application of AI in pathology pertains to its use as a tool for image analysis. This particular application holds the potential for automated diagnosis, efficient case prioritization to enhance workflow, and the generation of innovative findings.
